# Kafka 0.11.0.3 wire protocol

This document is the grammar that the `main` branch implements. It describes **only** what Apache Kafka 0.11.0.3
speaks — the last release of the 0.11 line, so it covers everything 0.11.0.0 added and the three bug-fix releases
that changed no wire format. `main` is the top of the cascade, so this is the **only** protocol document it carries:
the grammars of the lines below live on their own branches (`docs/protocol/0.10.2.md` on `0.10.x`,
`docs/protocol/0.9.0.md` on `0.9.x`, `docs/protocol/0.8.2.md` on `0.8.x`), while their wire vectors travel upwards
in [`vectors/`](vectors) and are replayed against the classes of this line — the strongest regression test the
repository has, because a 0.11.0.3 broker still speaks every one of those older versions.

Sources, in decreasing order of authority:

1. The broker itself (Apache Kafka 0.11.0.3, `docker/kafka-0.11.0.3`, four listeners on 9092–9095).
2. The 0.11.0.3 sources: `clients/src/main/java/org/apache/kafka/common/protocol/{ApiKeys,Errors,Protocol}.java`,
   `clients/src/main/java/org/apache/kafka/common/requests/*.java` and
   `clients/src/main/java/org/apache/kafka/common/record/*.java`, which are the authority for every api the broker
   parses with the schemas of the Java client — that is all of them but ControlledShutdown — plus
   `core/src/main/scala/kafka/server/KafkaApis.scala`, `core/src/main/scala/kafka/network/{SocketServer,RequestChannel}.scala`,
   `core/src/main/scala/kafka/coordinator/{group,transaction}/*.scala`, `core/src/main/scala/kafka/log/*.scala` and
   `core/src/main/scala/kafka/api/ControlledShutdownRequest.scala`. The Apache repository has **no `0.11.0.3`
   tag**; [`0.11.0.3-rc0`](https://github.com/apache/kafka/tree/0.11.0.3-rc0) is the commit the release was built
   from and is what every reference to "@ 0.11.0.3" in this repository means.
3. The wiki page [A Guide To The Kafka Protocol](https://cwiki.apache.org/confluence/display/KAFKA/A+Guide+To+The+Kafka+Protocol)
   and the KIPs of the 0.11 additions: KIP-98 (idempotent and transactional producer, record batch v2), KIP-82
   (record headers), KIP-101 (`OffsetForLeaderEpoch`), KIP-107 (`DeleteRecords`), KIP-124 (`throttle_time_ms`
   everywhere) and KIP-133 (`DescribeConfigs`/`AlterConfigs`). The wiki was written for 0.10 and never caught up
   with 0.11; where it disagrees with the container, the container wins.

Notation follows the wiki: productions are camel case, primitive types lower case, `[foo]` is an array of `foo`,
and a sequence of productions means concatenation.

Like the `0.10.x` line below it, this branch does not have to guess what its broker speaks: Kafka 0.10.0 added the
**ApiVersions** api (key 18), and the api-key table below is the literal answer of a real 0.11.0.3 broker to it.

## What 0.11.0.3 adds to the 0.10.2.2 protocol

Kafka 0.11.0.0 is the largest single raise of the protocol in this repository: it doubled the number of api keys,
gave every remaining api a `throttle_time_ms`, and replaced the message set with the **record batch v2**. The three
releases after it — 0.11.0.1, 0.11.0.2 and 0.11.0.3 — are bug fixes and change nothing on the wire, which is why
this line speaks the last of them.

| Area | 0.10.2.2 | 0.11.0.3 | KIP |
|---|---|---|---|
| Api keys | 0-20 | 0-33: **DeleteRecords 21, InitProducerId 22, OffsetForLeaderEpoch 23, AddPartitionsToTxn 24, AddOffsetsToTxn 25, EndTxn 26, WriteTxnMarkers 27, TxnOffsetCommit 28, DescribeAcls 29, CreateAcls 30, DeleteAcls 31, DescribeConfigs 32, AlterConfigs 33** | 98, 101, 107, 133 |
| Message format | v0, v1 (`MessageSet` of one `Message` per entry) | …and the **record batch v2** (magic 2): one CRC-32C per batch, varint-encoded records, delta-encoded offsets and timestamps, record **headers**, a producer id, epoch and sequence, and control batches. A topic keeps whatever `message.format.version` says, so all three formats are live | 98, 82 |
| Produce (0) | v0-v2 | **v3**: the request gains a leading `transactional_id` nullable string, the record set it carries is a batch v2, and the response partition gains `log_start_offset` int64 | 98 |
| Fetch (1) | v0-v3 | **v4**: the request gains `isolation_level` int8, the response partition gains `last_stable_offset` int64 and `[aborted_transactions]`. **v5**: both gain `log_start_offset` int64 | 98, 107 |
| Offsets (2) | v0, v1 | **v2**: the request gains `isolation_level` int8 after `replica_id`, the response a leading `throttle_time_ms` | 98, 124 |
| Metadata (3) | v0-v2 | **v3**: the response gains a leading `throttle_time_ms`. **v4**: the request gains `allow_auto_topic_creation` boolean | 124, 4 |
| ControlledShutdown (7) | **v1 only** as far as ApiVersions is concerned | unchanged — and the frame of v0 is still parsed, see the api-key table | – |
| OffsetCommit (8), OffsetFetch (9) | v0-v2 | **v3** each: a leading `throttle_time_ms` in the response | 124 |
| GroupCoordinator (10) | v0 | **v1**: the request gains `coordinator_type` int8 (0 = group, 1 = transaction), the response a leading `throttle_time_ms` and an `error_message` nullable string. Kafka calls the api **FindCoordinator** from 0.11 on; the class names of this repository keep `GroupCoordinator` | 98, 124 |
| JoinGroup (11), Heartbeat (12), LeaveGroup (13), SyncGroup (14), DescribeGroups (15), ListGroups (16) | v0/v1 | **one version more each** (JoinGroup v2, the others v1), all of them adding nothing but the leading `throttle_time_ms` of the response | 124 |
| SaslHandshake (17) | v0 | unchanged — the token exchange after the handshake is still raw, unframed data; `SaslAuthenticate` (key 36) is Kafka **1.0** | – |
| ApiVersions (18) | v0 | **v1**: the request is byte for byte the one of v0, the response gains a **trailing** `throttle_time_ms` — the one api that appends the field instead of prepending it | 124 |
| CreateTopics (19) | v0, v1 | **v2**: a leading `throttle_time_ms` in the response | 124 |
| DeleteTopics (20) | v0 | **v1**: a leading `throttle_time_ms` in the response | 124 |
| Producer semantics | at most once, blind retries | **idempotent** (`enable.idempotence`: a producer id, an epoch and a per-partition sequence deduplicate retries) and **transactional** (`transactional.id`, `initTransactions()`, `beginTransaction()`, `sendOffsetsToTransaction()`, `commitTransaction()`, `abortTransaction()`) | 98 |
| Consumer semantics | reads up to the high water mark | `isolation.level = read_committed` stops at the **last stable offset** and drops the records of aborted transactions | 98 |
| Error codes | -1 … 44 | -1 … **55**: 45-47 the idempotent producer, 48-53 the transactions, 54-55 the ACL apis. (**56** `KAFKA_STORAGE_ERROR` is Kafka 1.0) | 98 |
| An unparseable frame | the broker closes the connection | unchanged | – |

Everything else in this document is the 0.10.2.2 grammar, unchanged and re-verified against a 0.11.0.3 broker.

## Implementation model

Protocol classes do not hand-roll `pack()`/`unpack()` calls: every message and DTO declares its binary layout and the
engine reads and writes it. The design came up the cascade from `0.10.x`, which is why the shape of a class here is
the shape it has on the three lines below.

* `Protocol\Kafka\Protocol\BinarySchemaInterface::getScheme(): array` maps a property name to its type.
* `Protocol\Kafka\Protocol\BinarySchema` is the engine: `readObjectFromStream()`, `writeObjectToStream()`,
  `readSingleType()`, `writeSingleType()`, `getObjectTypeSize()`.
* A request extends its parent scheme: `parent::getScheme() + ['topics' => [BinarySchema::TYPE_STRING]]`.
* Arrays are `[ItemType]` for a plain list, `['keyField' => ItemClass::class]` for a list indexed by one of the item
  fields, and gain `BinarySchema::FLAG_NULLABLE => true` when `-1` is a valid count.

The type set of the apis is the one of 0.10: `TYPE_INT8`, `TYPE_INT16`, `TYPE_INT32`, `TYPE_INT64`, `TYPE_BOOLEAN`,
`TYPE_STRING`, `TYPE_NULLABLE_STRING`, `TYPE_BYTEARRAY` (nullable) and int32-prefixed arrays. A *request* can carry
a nullable array since 0.10 (the topics of Metadata v1 and of OffsetFetch v2), so `null` written as `ff ff ff ff`
and `[]` written as `00 00 00 00` are two different requests here.

Kafka 0.11 adds one family on top of it, and **only inside a record batch v2** — no request or response body of any
api of this line uses it:

* `TYPE_VARINT_ZIGZAG` (11) — an int32 written zigzag-encoded, 7 bits per byte;
* `TYPE_VARLONG_ZIGZAG` (12) — the same for an int64;
* `TYPE_VARCHAR_ZIGZAG` (13) — a byte array whose length is such a varint, `-1` (`01`) meaning `null`;
* `FLAG_VARARRAY` (14) — an array whose element count is a varint instead of an int32.

`Common\Utils\ByteUtils` holds the zigzag and CRC-32C helpers of `org.apache.kafka.common.utils.ByteUtils`, and
`Stream::readVarint()`/`writeVarint()` the byte loop. **The numbers 5, 6 and 7 stay reserved**: they were
`TYPE_VARINT`, `TYPE_VARLONG` and `TYPE_VARCHAR` of the pre-schema `main`, which wrote *raw* (non-zigzag) varints —
an encoding that appears nowhere on the wire of Kafka 0.11 — and they are left free so that a reader of an old
scheme cannot mistake one for the other.

An api that has more than one version and whose layout changes between them is described by one class per version:
a scheme is a static property of a class, so `OffsetCommitRequest` (the highest version) and `OffsetCommitRequestV0`
differ in their `VERSION` constant, which the version-aware `getScheme()` of the request and of its DTOs follows.
`XRequest` is always the highest version the broker of this line serves, and every `XRequestV<n>` below it only
lowers that constant.

Two defects of the engine of the pre-schema `main` are fixed here: `TYPE_INT8` is read as a **signed** value (the
attributes byte of a message and the error codes are signed), and `TYPE_BYTEARRAY` round-trips `null` as the int32
`-1` that the specification prescribes.

## Protocol primitive types

| Type    | Encoding                                                                         |
|---------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| `int8`  | 1 byte, signed                                                                    |
| `int16` | 2 bytes, signed, big endian                                                       |
| `int32` | 4 bytes, signed, big endian                                                       |
| `int64` | 8 bytes, signed, big endian (two's complement)                                    |
| `boolean` | 1 byte: `00` is false, **any** non-zero byte is true; this client writes `01`   |
| `string`| `int16` length `N` followed by `N` bytes of UTF-8; `N = -1` (`ff ff`) means `null` |
| `bytes` | `int32` length `N` followed by `N` bytes; `N = -1` (`ff ff ff ff`) means `null`    |
| `[foo]` | `int32` element count `N` followed by `N` repetitions of `foo`                     |
| `varint` | **Kafka 0.11, record batch v2 only.** An int32 zigzag-encoded (`0 → 0, -1 → 1, 1 → 2, -2 → 3 …`) and written 7 bits per byte, least significant group first, the high bit set on every byte but the last: 1 to 5 bytes (`ByteUtils.writeVarint` @ 0.11.0.3). `TYPE_VARINT_ZIGZAG` of the engine, `Common\Utils\ByteUtils` for the zigzag step |
| `varlong` | The same encoding of an int64, 1 to 10 bytes. `TYPE_VARLONG_ZIGZAG` |
| varint `bytes` | `varint` length `N` followed by `N` bytes; `N = -1` (`01`) means `null` - the key, value and headers of a record. `TYPE_VARCHAR_ZIGZAG` |
| varint `[foo]` | `varint` element count `N` followed by `N` repetitions of `foo` - the headers of a record. `FLAG_VARARRAY` (the count is zigzag-encoded too) |

Byte-exact examples, as implemented by `Protocol\Kafka\IO\Stream`:

| Value                     | Bytes                     |
|---------------------------|---------------------------|
| `int16` `-1`              | `ff ff`                   |
| `int16` `-32768`          | `80 00`                   |
| `int32` `-1`              | `ff ff ff ff`             |
| `int64` `-1`              | `ff ff ff ff ff ff ff ff` |
| `string` `null`           | `ff ff`                   |
| `string` `""`             | `00 00`                   |
| `string` `"test"`         | `00 04 74 65 73 74`       |
| `bytes` `null`            | `ff ff ff ff`             |
| `bytes` `""`              | `00 00 00 00`             |
| `boolean` `false` / `true`| `00` / `01`               |
| empty array               | `00 00 00 00`             |
| null array                | `ff ff ff ff`             |
| `varint` `0` / `-1` / `1` | `00` / `01` / `02`        |
| `varint` `64` / `-65`     | `80 01` / `81 01`         |
| `varint` `300`            | `d8 04`                   |
| `varint` `2147483647`     | `fe ff ff ff 0f`          |
| `varint` `-2147483648`    | `ff ff ff ff 0f`          |
| `varlong` `2^31`          | `80 80 80 80 10`          |
| `varlong` `PHP_INT_MAX`   | `fe ff ff ff ff ff ff ff ff 01` |
| varint `bytes` `null` / `""` / `de ad be ef` | `01` / `00` / `08 de ad be ef` |

An empty string and a null string are different values on the wire; the client id of a request, for example, is always
an empty string and never null.

## Common request and response structure

Every request and every response is framed by its size:

```
RequestOrResponse => Size (RequestMessage | ResponseMessage)
  Size => int32
```

`Size` is the length in bytes of everything that follows it. A client reads a response by first reading these four
bytes as an integer `N` and then reading exactly `N` more bytes.

### Requests

```
RequestMessage => ApiKey ApiVersion CorrelationId ClientId RequestBody
  ApiKey        => int16
  ApiVersion    => int16
  CorrelationId => int32
  ClientId      => string
```

* `ApiKey` — numeric id of the API being invoked (see the table below).
* `ApiVersion` — version of that API; the broker answers in exactly the format that matches the requested version.
* `CorrelationId` — user-supplied value that the broker passes back unmodified.
* `ClientId` — user-supplied identifier of the client application.

A Metadata request v0 with client id `test`, correlation id `1` and an empty topic list is therefore:

```
00 00 00 12   Size = 18
00 03         ApiKey = 3 (Metadata)
00 00         ApiVersion = 0
00 00 00 01   CorrelationId = 1
00 04 74 65 73 74   ClientId = "test"
00 00 00 00   [TopicName] = empty
```

### Responses

```
Response => Size CorrelationId ResponseBody
  Size          => int32
  CorrelationId => int32
```

The broker guarantees that responses on a single connection come back in the order the requests were sent, and every
response repeats the correlation id of its request.

There is exactly one exception to the request/response symmetry: a Produce request with `RequiredAcks = 0` gets no
response at all.

## API keys

From `clients/src/main/java/org/apache/kafka/common/protocol/ApiKeys.java` @ 0.11.0.3, which ends at key 33.
Identifiers follow the pre-schema `main` branch wherever the concept exists on both, only the wire format is
0.11. Api key 10 is therefore called **GroupCoordinator** here, although the 0.11 sources renamed it to
`FindCoordinator`; the 0.8.2 sources called it `ConsumerMetadataRequest`.

The table below is not a guess but the literal answer of the container to an **ApiVersions** request (key 18),
decoded by [`tests/Integration/ApiVersionProbeTest.php`](../../tests/Integration/ApiVersionProbeTest.php) and stored
as the wire vectors `apiversions.response.v0` and `apiversions.response.v1`. The same test sends a real frame of
every key and version of the table and checks that the broker really answers it, and one frame above every one of
them to check that it really does not.

The "Implemented here" column lists the versions this branch has a class for; the one in **bold** is the version
the client sends. A cell that names a ticket (`T3`, `T4`, …) is a version this line will serve when that ticket of
the 0.11 epic lands, and the ticket that owns it fills the row in.

| ApiKey | Name                              | Versions served | Client-facing | Implemented here |
|--------|-----------------------------------|-----------------|---------------|------------------|
| 0      | Produce (`ProducerRequest`)       | v0 … v3         | yes           | v0, v1, v2, **v3** (**v2** for `message.format.version` below 0.11.0) |
| 1      | Fetch                             | v0 … v5         | yes           | v0, v1, v2, v3, v4, **v5** |
| 2      | Offsets (`OffsetRequest`)         | v0 … v2         | yes           | v0, v1, **v2**   |
| 3      | Metadata (`TopicMetadataRequest`) | v0 … v4         | yes           | v0, v1, v2, v3, **v4** |
| 4      | LeaderAndIsr                      | v0              | broker→broker | no               |
| 5      | StopReplica                       | v0              | broker→broker | no               |
| 6      | UpdateMetadata                    | v0 … v3         | broker→broker | no               |
| 7      | ControlledShutdown                | **v1** (v0 still parsed, see below) | controller | v0, **v1** |
| 8      | OffsetCommit                      | v0 … v3         | yes           | v0, v1, v2, **v3** (**v0** for `offsets.storage = zookeeper`) |
| 9      | OffsetFetch                       | v0 … v3         | yes           | v0, v1, v2, **v3** (**v0** for `offsets.storage = zookeeper`) |
| 10     | GroupCoordinator (FindCoordinator)| v0, v1          | yes           | v0, **v1**       |
| 11     | JoinGroup                         | v0 … v2         | yes           | v0, v1, **v2**   |
| 12     | Heartbeat                         | v0, v1          | yes           | v0, **v1**       |
| 13     | LeaveGroup                        | v0, v1          | yes           | v0, **v1**       |
| 14     | SyncGroup                         | v0, v1          | yes           | v0, **v1**       |
| 15     | DescribeGroups                    | v0, v1          | yes           | v0, **v1**       |
| 16     | ListGroups                        | v0, v1          | yes           | v0, **v1**       |
| 17     | SaslHandshake                     | v0              | yes           | **v0**           |
| 18     | ApiVersions                       | v0, v1          | yes           | v0, **v1**       |
| 19     | CreateTopics                      | v0 … v2         | controller    | v0, v1, **v2**   |
| 20     | DeleteTopics                      | v0, v1          | controller    | v0, **v1**       |
| 21     | DeleteRecords                     | v0              | yes           | **v0**           |
| 22     | InitProducerId                    | v0              | yes           | **v0**           |
| 23     | OffsetForLeaderEpoch              | v0              | broker→broker | **v0** (classes and vectors, no client method) |
| 24     | AddPartitionsToTxn                | v0              | yes           | **v0**           |
| 25     | AddOffsetsToTxn                   | v0              | yes           | **v0**           |
| 26     | EndTxn                            | v0              | yes           | **v0**           |
| 27     | WriteTxnMarkers                   | v0              | broker→broker | **v0** (classes and vectors, no client method) |
| 28     | TxnOffsetCommit                   | v0              | yes           | **v0**           |
| 29     | DescribeAcls                      | v0              | yes           | no, see below    |
| 30     | CreateAcls                        | v0              | yes           | no, see below    |
| 31     | DeleteAcls                        | v0              | yes           | no, see below    |
| 32     | DescribeConfigs                   | v0              | yes           | **v0**           |
| 33     | AlterConfigs                      | v0              | yes           | **v0**           |

The keys 4, 5, 6 and 27 are answered by a 0.11 broker like any other request; the probe sends 4, 5 and 6 with the
controller epoch `-1`, which is stale, so the broker answers the error code 11 (`StaleControllerEpoch`) and changes
nothing, and 27 with an empty marker array, which it answers with an empty array of its own.

**Key 7 is the one row whose minimum version is not 0.** ControlledShutdown v0 uses a request header *without* a
client id, which the Java client of 0.11 cannot build any more, so the api reports `MinVersion = 1`. The frame of
v0 is nevertheless still accepted, and so is any version above 1 — see "The last Scala api does not check its
version" below.

**The set of keys is not a constant of the release.** `ApiVersionsResponse.createApiVersionsResponse()` @ 0.11.0.3
builds the answer from `ApiKeys.values()` filtered by `apiKey.minRequiredInterBrokerMagic <= minMagic`, where
`minMagic` is the message format that `inter.broker.protocol.version` implies. The container runs the default
0.11.0 format (magic 2), so every key appears; a 0.11 broker rolled back to `inter.broker.protocol.version=0.10.2`
answers with fewer. A client must therefore read the table it gets and not the one printed here.

**The ACL apis 29, 30 and 31 are not implemented on this line** (decision 3 of the epic): they only do anything on
a broker configured with an `authorizer.class.name`, and this repository captures every wire vector from a real
broker. Without an authorizer a 0.11.0.3 broker answers all three with the error code **54**
(`SecurityDisabled`) — it does answer them, which is what the probe checks.

### An api the broker does not serve closes the connection

**This is the behaviour that changed in Kafka 0.10 and that 0.11 keeps unchanged, and it is the one a client of the
two lowest lines gets wrong.** A 0.9.0.1 broker silently *dropped* a frame it could not parse and kept the
connection open; a 0.10.2.2 or 0.11.0.3 broker **closes the connection**.

`RequestChannel.Request` @ 0.11.0.3 parses every incoming frame with
`AbstractRequest.getRequest(apiKey, apiVersion, buffer)` and wraps whatever that throws — an unknown api key, a
version `ApiKeys.schemaFor()` has no schema for, or a body that does not match the schema of the version it claims —
into an `InvalidRequestException`. `SocketServer.processCompletedReceives` catches that and the `SchemaException` of
the schema layer and closes the channel. Both frames below were sent to the container of this line:

```
ERROR Closing socket for 172.18.0.2:9092-172.18.0.1:47628 because of error (kafka.network.Processor)
org.apache.kafka.common.errors.InvalidRequestException: Error getting request for apiKey: 8 and apiVersion: 4
Caused by: java.lang.IllegalArgumentException: Invalid version for API key OFFSET_COMMIT: 4
	at org.apache.kafka.common.protocol.ApiKeys.schemaFor(ApiKeys.java:173)
```

```
ERROR Closing socket for 172.18.0.2:9092-172.18.0.1:47642 because of error (kafka.network.Processor)
org.apache.kafka.common.errors.InvalidRequestException: Error getting request for apiKey: 11 and apiVersion: 2
Caused by: org.apache.kafka.common.protocol.types.SchemaException: Error reading field 'member_id': Error reading
string of length 25455, only 10 bytes available
```

The second example is a JoinGroup **v2** frame carrying a **v0** body: the key and the version are served, the bytes
behind them are not, and the connection is lost just the same. A malformed body is therefore as expensive as an
unknown api. Note the first one: the version of the *error* moved with the release. On 0.10.2.2 the connection died
for OffsetCommit **v3**, which a 0.11.0.3 broker serves; the frame that dies here is v4, the version Kafka 2.0
added. The same holds for every api this line raised, which is exactly why the test that pins this behaviour derives
its frames from the served api table instead of hard-coding version numbers.

For a client this means:

* The answer to "why did my request never come back" is "the broker hung up", not "the broker dropped it". A
  read on that connection ends in the end of the stream, which this client reports as `NetworkException` (code 13),
  not in a request timeout.
* Only that one connection is lost. The broker itself is unaffected, and the next connection is answered normally.
* A client must never send a version it has not seen in an ApiVersions answer. This branch therefore hard-codes the
  versions of 0.11.0.3 and `ApiKeys` stops at 33.

There are exactly **two** exceptions, and both are verified by the probe:

**ApiVersions answers an unknown version instead of closing.** `RequestChannel.Request` special-cases key 18: for a
version `Protocol.apiVersionSupported()` rejects it builds a dummy request, so that
`KafkaApis.handleApiVersionsRequest` can answer `ApiVersionsResponse.unsupportedVersionSend()` — the error code
**35** with an empty api array, written in the layout of **version 0** no matter which version was asked for. The
connection survives, which is what makes the answer usable: a client that guessed too high reads the 35 and retries
with a version it knows. This is the whole reason the api can be the first request on a connection.

**ControlledShutdown answers every version**, see below.

### The last Scala api does not check its version

Kafka 0.10 moved every api but one to the schemas of the Java client, which validate the version, and 0.11 did not
finish the job either. The exception is **ControlledShutdown (key 7)**: `RequestChannel.Request` @ 0.11.0.3 still
calls `ControlledShutdownRequest.readFrom(buffer)` for that key before the request header is parsed at all — the
source still carries the comment *"this will be removed once we remove support for v0 of ControlledShutdownRequest
(which depends on a non-standard request header)"* — and that parser only asks whether the version is above 0, to
decide whether a client id follows:

```scala
val versionId     = buffer.getShort
val correlationId = buffer.getInt
val clientId      = if (versionId > 0) Some(readShortString(buffer)) else None
val brokerId      = buffer.getInt
```

A 0.11.0.3 broker therefore answers key 7 with **any** version — the retired v0 without a client id, the v1 of the
table, and v2 or above like v1 — although ApiVersions reports the single version 1 for it. Nothing may be read into
that: only version 1 is the protocol of 0.11.0.3, the rest is an accident of the last Scala parser, and this client
sends v1. The v0 class is kept for the `0.8.x`/`0.9.x` vectors.

Every other key of the table validates its version, including the thirteen that Kafka 0.11 added: a 0.11.0.3 broker
closes the connection for Produce v4, Fetch v6, Offsets v3, Metadata v5, OffsetCommit v4, JoinGroup v3,
DeleteRecords v1, InitProducerId v1, EndTxn v1 and every other "one above the table" frame alike, and for the api
key 34 (`AlterReplicaLogDirs`, Kafka 1.0) as well.

### What is not in Kafka 0.11.0.3

Everything below is Kafka **1.0 or later**, or a deliberate omission of this line, and must not appear here:

* **`SaslAuthenticate` (api key 36)**, Kafka 1.0 — the SASL token exchange of this line is still raw, unframed data
  on the socket after the SaslHandshake, without a request header of its own. `AlterReplicaLogDirs` (34) and
  `DescribeLogDirs` (35) are 1.0 as well, so `ApiKeys` stops at **33** here.
* **The error code 56** (`KAFKA_STORAGE_ERROR`) and everything above it: `Errors.java` @ 0.11.0.3 ends at **55**.
  `KafkaException::fromCode()` answers an unknown code with `UnknownErrorException`, which is what a client should
  do with a code it has never heard of.
* **Fetch v6, Produce v4, Metadata v5 and the other versions of Kafka 1.0 and 2.0** — the api-key table above is the
  ceiling, and a frame above it costs the connection.
* **`DeleteGroups`, `ElectLeaders`, incremental `AlterConfigs`, the `KIP-98` broker-side changes of Kafka 1.x** and
  every other api key above 33.
* **The ACL apis `DescribeAcls` (29), `CreateAcls` (30) and `DeleteAcls` (31)**, which a 0.11.0.3 broker does serve
  but which this line leaves unimplemented by decision: they need a broker with an `authorizer.class.name`, and
  every wire vector of this repository comes from a real broker (see the api-key table).
* **Flexible versions, tagged fields and the `ApiVersions` v3 handshake** of Kafka 2.4 — the header of this line is
  the plain `api_key api_version correlation_id client_id` of every version below it.

## ApiVersions API (key 18, v0 and v1)

Asks a broker which api keys and versions it serves. Kafka 0.10.0 added it, and it is the only request whose answer
tells a client what the peer on the other side speaks; on the two lowest lines of this repository there is no such
api, and the api-key table had to be probed frame by frame. Kafka 0.11 raised it to **version 1**, the version this
client sends.

```
ApiVersionsRequest =>
```

The request has **no body at all** in either version — `API_VERSIONS_REQUEST_V1 = API_VERSIONS_REQUEST_V0` in
`Protocol.java` @ 0.11.0.3 — so the whole frame is 4 bytes of `Size` plus 8 bytes of header plus the client id, and
the only difference between a v0 and a v1 request is the `ApiVersion` field of the header.

```
ApiVersionsResponse (Version: 0) => ErrorCode [ApiKey MinVersion MaxVersion]
  ErrorCode  => int16
  ApiKey     => int16
  MinVersion => int16
  MaxVersion => int16

ApiVersionsResponse (Version: 1) => ErrorCode [ApiKey MinVersion MaxVersion] ThrottleTimeMs
  ThrottleTimeMs => int32
```

* `ErrorCode` belongs to the whole answer and is `0` or **35** (`UnsupportedVersion`).
* One entry per api the broker knows, in ascending order of `ApiKey`, including the broker-to-broker keys 4, 5, 6
  and 27 that no client ever sends. Both version bounds are **inclusive**, and the entry itself has no version of
  its own: `API_VERSIONS_RESPONSE_V1` reuses the `API_VERSIONS_V0` schema for it.
* A 0.11.0.3 broker answers with the 34 keys 0 to 33 of the api-key table above. A client must not assume that set —
  the whole point of the api is that a later broker reports more and an older one reports fewer, and even one
  0.11.0.3 broker reports fewer when its `inter.broker.protocol.version` is rolled back (see the api-key table).

**`ThrottleTimeMs` closes this answer instead of opening it.** KIP-124 gave fifteen apis of Kafka 0.11 a throttle
time and put it in front of the body in every one of them — Metadata v3, OffsetCommit v3, JoinGroup v2, the group
apis, CreateTopics v2, DeleteTopics v1 — with exactly one exception, this api, where `API_VERSIONS_RESPONSE_V1`
appends it. The reason is the paragraph below: the answer to a version the broker cannot parse is written in the
version 0 layout, so the `ErrorCode` has to sit at the same offset in both versions or a client that guessed too
high could not read it. Without a `request_percentage` quota the field is 0, and an ApiVersions request is the last
request a quota would ever throttle.

`Protocol\Kafka\Protocol\Request\ApiVersionsResponse` indexes the array by `ApiKey`, so a caller asks for one api
directly (`supports(ApiKeys::FETCH, 5)`, `maxVersionOf(ApiKeys::METADATA)`); `ApiVersionsResponseV0` is the same
class with the trailing field switched off, and `ApiVersionsRequestV0` the same request with the lower version in
its header. `Client::apiVersions(Node $node)` returns the whole response and `Admin\AdminClient::getApiVersions(Node $node)`
the indexed array; every broker answers for itself, so a rolling upgrade shows up as brokers with different ranges.

**This client does not negotiate with the answer.** Like the three lines below it, it sends the fixed versions a
broker of its own Kafka release serves — the "Implemented here" column of the api-key table — and the api is there
to report what the peer is, not to choose a version at runtime.

Three properties make the request usable as the *first* frame on a connection, and all three are verified against
the container:

1. **An unknown version is answered, not punished.** Every other key or version a 0.11.0.3 broker cannot parse costs
   the connection; ApiVersions is special-cased in `RequestChannel.Request`, so a version it does not serve comes
   back as the error code 35 with an **empty** api array, in the layout of **version 0**, and the connection stays
   open. `ApiVersionsResponse.unsupportedVersionSend()` writes that frame with the hard-coded version `0`, which is
   why the answer to a version 7 request has no throttle time even though the broker serves version 1. A client that
   guessed too high therefore learns the truth instead of having to reconnect.
2. **It is answered before authentication.** On a SASL listener the broker answers ApiVersions while the connection
   is still unauthenticated (`KafkaApis.handleApiVersionsRequest` says so in a comment), so a client can learn the
   surface of a broker before it knows whether it may talk to it at all. On an SSL listener the TLS handshake still
   comes first.
3. **It is cheap.** The request carries no state, changes nothing, and the answer of a 0.11.0.3 broker is 218 bytes
   in version 1 (214 in version 0) with a 23-character client id.

A broker of a lower line answers fewer keys, and one older than 0.10 has no key 18 at all: a **0.9.0.1** broker
drops the frame and never answers it, a **0.8.2.2** broker closes the socket. "No answer at all" is therefore how a
client recognises a pre-0.10 peer — there is no error code for it. A peer between 0.10.0 and 0.10.2.2 answers the
version 1 request of this line with the error code 35 in the version 0 layout, and has to be asked again with
`ApiVersionsRequestV0`.

## MessageSet and Message

The message set format is shared by the Produce and Fetch APIs and by the on-disk log. Note that a `MessageSet` is
**not** preceded by an `int32` element count like a regular array — it is a raw byte region whose length is given by
the surrounding `MessageSetSize` field.

Kafka 0.10.0 added **message format v1** (KIP-31 and KIP-32) next to the format v0 of 0.8 and 0.9. A 0.10.2.2 broker
reads and writes both, and the `MagicByte` of a message says which one it is in — it is the discriminator that has to
be read before the rest of the message:

```
MessageSet => [Offset MessageSize Message]
  Offset      => int64
  MessageSize => int32

Message (MagicByte = 0) => Crc MagicByte Attributes Key Value
  Crc        => int32
  MagicByte  => int8
  Attributes => int8
  Key        => bytes
  Value      => bytes

Message (MagicByte = 1) => Crc MagicByte Attributes Timestamp Key Value
  Crc        => int32
  MagicByte  => int8
  Attributes => int8
  Timestamp  => int64
  Key        => bytes
  Value      => bytes
```

* `Crc` — CRC-32 (IEEE, the same polynomial as `crc32()` in PHP) of the message bytes that follow the CRC field,
  i.e. of `MagicByte Attributes [Timestamp] Key Value`. The `Timestamp` of a v1 message is part of the checksum.
* `Attributes`:

  | Bits | Meaning | Values |
  |------|---------|--------|
  | 0–2  | compression codec | 0 none, 1 gzip, 2 snappy, **3 lz4** |
  | 3    | timestamp type (v1 only) | 0 `CreateTime`, 1 `LogAppendTime` |
  | 4–7  | reserved, always 0 | |

  All four codecs are implemented by this client. In a message of format v0 bit 3 is reserved and stays 0.
* `Timestamp` — milliseconds since the epoch, or **-1** (`Message.NoTimestamp`) for a message that carries none. The
  broker accepts -1 and stores it as it is; `message.timestamp.difference.max.ms` (unlimited by default) is what
  bounds a `CreateTime` that is far away from the clock of the broker, and a violation is answered with
  **32 `InvalidTimestamp`**.
* The timestamp type is a property of the **topic** (`message.timestamp.type`, `CreateTime` by default): with
  `LogAppendTime` the broker overwrites the timestamp of every message it appends with its own clock and sets bit 3
  of the attributes.
* A compressed message is a regular message whose `Value` is a complete, compressed `MessageSet`.
* The server is allowed to return a **partial message at the end of a message set**; clients must silently drop a
  trailing message that is shorter than its announced size. The size of the smallest possible message —
  `MinMessageOverhead`, 14 bytes — is the one of a format v0 message without key and value, and a `MessageSize`
  below it means a corrupt set (a format v1 message without key and value is 22 bytes).

### Offsets inside a compressed message set

The `Offset` of the entries of a *produced* set is ignored: the broker assigns the real offsets when it appends the
set, so a producer numbers its messages 0, 1, 2 … Inside a **compressed** set the two message formats differ:

* **v0** — the broker rewrites the offset of every inner message with the absolute offset of the log.
* **v1** — the inner offsets stay **relative** (0, 1, 2 …) and only the offset of the **wrapper** message is
  rewritten, with the absolute offset of the *last* inner message. A reader has to decompress the whole set to see
  the last relative offset before it can translate the others:

      absolute = wrapperOffset - lastInnerOffset + innerOffset

  (`ByteBufferMessageSet.scala` and `RecordsIterator.DeepRecordsIterator` @ 0.10.2.2). The reason is streaming
  compression: the relative offset of an inner message is known while it is written, its absolute one is not.
  The vectors `messageformat.v1.createtime.gzip` and `messageformat.v0.lz4.broken-checksum` below are the same three
  records at the offsets 3, 4 and 5 in both formats: relative `0, 1, 2` under a wrapper at offset 5 in v1, absolute
  `3, 4, 5` under the very same wrapper offset in v0.

### Timestamps inside a compressed message set

The wrapper message of a v1 set carries the timestamp of the whole batch, and it is the only message whose
attributes are meaningful:

* the timestamp type of the wrapper applies to **every** message of the set;
* with `CreateTime` the wrapper carries the **largest** timestamp of its inner messages, and every inner message
  keeps its own;
* with `LogAppendTime` the wrapper carries the append time of the batch and the timestamps of the inner messages are
  **ignored** — the broker does not even rewrite them, as `messageformat.v1.logappendtime.gzip` below shows: the
  wrapper announces `0x09` (gzip + LogAppendTime) and the append time, while the compressed inner messages still
  hold the `CreateTime` values of the producer;
* a producer must write `CreateTime` in the attributes of every inner message, otherwise the broker rejects the set.

### The lz4 codec and the KAFKA-3160 checksum

The `Value` of an lz4 message is an **LZ4 frame** (the v1.5.1 frame format), as `KafkaLZ4BlockOutputStream` writes
it: the magic number `04 22 4d 18`, the `FLG` byte `0x60` (version 1, independent blocks, no checksums), the `BD`
byte `0x40` (64 KiB blocks), the header checksum `HC`, then `int32` little-endian block lengths with their blocks,
and a block length of `0` as the end mark. The highest bit of a block length marks a block that is stored
uncompressed because compressing it made it bigger.

`HC` is the second byte of the xxHash32 of the frame descriptor, `(xxh32(FLG BD) >> 8) & 0xff` = `0x82`. Until
Kafka 0.10.0 the clients hashed the magic number **together with** the descriptor, which gives `0x1a`
([KAFKA-3160](https://issues.apache.org/jira/browse/KAFKA-3160)). The fix could not be applied to the messages that
older clients still have to read, so the broken checksum became part of the format of **message format v0** and the
correct one is used from format v1 on (`MemoryRecordsBuilder.wrapForOutput()`:
`useBrokenFlagDescriptorChecksum = magic == 0`). A reader has to accept both, which is what this client does — the
0.10.2.2 broker itself writes `04 22 4d 18 60 40 1a` in every answer it down-converts to format v0.

### What the broker converts, and when

The message format of the **log** is the `message.format.version` of the topic (`log.message.format.version` of the
broker by default, `0.10.2` on the container of this branch), and the broker converts whatever it is given:

| Producer writes | Topic format | Stored as | Fetch v0/v1 answers | Fetch v2 answers |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| v1 | 0.10.x | v1, timestamps kept | **v0**, no timestamps, absolute inner offsets | v1, as stored |
| v0 | 0.10.x | **v1**, `Timestamp` = -1 (`NoTimestamp`) | v0 | v1 with -1 timestamps |
| v1 | 0.9.0 | **v0**, timestamps dropped | v0 | v0 |
| v0 | 0.9.0 | v0 | v0 | v0 |

The down-conversion of the answer is what `KafkaApis.handleFetchRequest` does for a client that asks with a Fetch
request below **version 2**: `versionId <= 1 && getMagic(tp) > 0` ⇒ `records.toMessageFormat(MAGIC_VALUE_V0)`. It
strips the timestamps, turns the relative inner offsets into absolute ones and recompresses the set with the same
codec — with the broken lz4 checksum, since the result is a format v0 set. Version 2 of the Fetch API is *only* the
statement "I understand message format v1"; its request and its response are byte-identical to version 1.

A client of this branch therefore sees timestamps only through a Fetch request of version 2 or above, which is what
`Client::fetch()` sends (v3); `Record::$timestamp` is `null` and `Record::$timestampType` is `NO_TIMESTAMP_TYPE` for
everything that arrives in format v0 - a topic of the older `message.format.version`, or any partition read with an
explicitly built `FetchRequestV1`/`FetchRequestV0`.

## RecordBatch (message format v2)

Kafka 0.11 replaced the message set with the **record batch** (KIP-98 for the format, KIP-82 for the headers). It
is the third message format of the protocol, the `MagicByte` 2, and it is what the log of a topic with the default
`message.format.version=0.11.0` holds and what a Produce v3 and a Fetch v4/v5 carry. A byte region of a partition is
still not a protocol array — it has no element count, only the `MessageSetSize`/`RecordSetSize` of the field around
it — but the entries of that region are now batches instead of messages:

```
RecordBatch =>
  BaseOffset           => int64
  BatchLength          => int32
  PartitionLeaderEpoch => int32
  Magic                => int8 (2)
  Crc                  => int32 (CRC-32C, unsigned)
  Attributes           => int16
  LastOffsetDelta      => int32
  FirstTimestamp       => int64
  MaxTimestamp         => int64
  ProducerId           => int64
  ProducerEpoch        => int16
  BaseSequence         => int32
  RecordCount          => int32
  Records              => the RecordCount records, compressed as one block when a codec is announced

Record =>
  Length         => varint
  Attributes     => int8
  TimestampDelta => varlong
  OffsetDelta    => varint
  Key            => varint-prefixed bytes
  Value          => varint-prefixed bytes
  Headers        => varint-counted [Header]

Header =>
  HeaderKeyLength   => varint
  HeaderKey         => string
  HeaderValueLength => varint
  HeaderValue       => bytes
```

Every length and both deltas are **zigzag varints** (`TYPE_VARINT_ZIGZAG`, `TYPE_VARLONG_ZIGZAG`,
`TYPE_VARCHAR_ZIGZAG` of the engine, see "Protocol primitive types"), a length of -1 (`01`) is `null` and a length
of 0 (`00`) is the empty byte array. The `Attributes` of a *record* are unused in 0.11 and always 0; the count of the
headers is a varint too, and a header key is a UTF-8 string that is never null, while a header value may be.

The fixed part of a batch is exactly **61 bytes** (`RECORD_BATCH_OVERHEAD`), from `BaseOffset` through
`RecordCount`; `BatchLength` counts everything after itself, so a batch occupies `12 + BatchLength` bytes, the same
`Offset`/`MessageSize` shape a message set entry has. That is not an accident: the `MagicByte` sits at **offset 16**
of an entry in all three formats — after the 8-byte offset, the 4-byte length and either the CRC of a message or the
`PartitionLeaderEpoch` of a batch — which is what lets a reader look at the format of a region before it decides how
to parse it. `MemoryRecords` of this client does exactly that, entry by entry, and hands each entry to `MessageSet`
or to `RecordBatch`.

### Attributes

| Bits | Meaning | Values |
|------|---------|--------|
| 0–2  | compression codec | 0 none, 1 gzip, 2 snappy, 3 lz4 |
| 3    | timestamp type | 0 `CreateTime`, 1 `LogAppendTime` |
| 4    | transactional | 1 when the batch belongs to a transaction (KIP-98) |
| 5    | control batch | 1 when the records are markers of the transaction protocol |
| 6–15 | reserved, always 0 | |

The field is an `int16` now, and only its low byte is used — `DefaultRecordBatch.attributes()` reads the `int16` and
casts it to a `byte`. A batch of the format v2 always announces a timestamp type: unlike a message of the format v0,
it has no "no timestamp type" state.

### The checksum is a CRC-32C, and it does not cover the whole batch

`Crc` is the **CRC-32C** (Castagnoli, RFC 3720 section B.4 — `hash('crc32c')` of PHP, `Crc32C.java` of the broker),
not the CRC-32 (IEEE) of a message, and it covers the batch from the `Attributes` field to its end, i.e. everything
that follows the checksum itself. Three fields are therefore *outside* of it:

* `BaseOffset` — the broker assigns the real offsets when it appends the batch;
* `BatchLength` — it does not change;
* `PartitionLeaderEpoch` — assigned by the leader on append (KIP-101), and the whole point of leaving it out of the
  checksum is that a broker must not have to recompute the checksum of every batch it receives.

The checksum is also *behind* the magic byte, which is what makes reading the magic first mandatory: in the message
formats v0 and v1 the CRC comes first and the magic second.

### Offsets, timestamps and sequence numbers are deltas

A record stores none of its three numbers in full, only the difference to the header of its batch:

    offset    = BaseOffset     + OffsetDelta
    timestamp = FirstTimestamp + TimestampDelta
    sequence  = BaseSequence   + OffsetDelta

`LastOffsetDelta` is the delta of the last record, so `BaseOffset + LastOffsetDelta` is the last offset of the batch
and `BaseSequence + LastOffsetDelta` its last sequence number (wrapping at the largest int32, as
`DefaultRecordBatch.incrementSequence()` does). Both survive log compaction even when the records they describe are
removed, because the broker rebuilds the state of a producer from them; that is why a batch may hold **zero**
records and still exist, and why `RecordCount` is a plain `int32` outside the compressed block.

`FirstTimestamp` is always the `CreateTime` of the first record, *even in a `LogAppendTime` batch*: with
`LogAppendTime` the broker only overwrites `MaxTimestamp` with the time of the append and sets bit 3, and every
record of the batch reads that `MaxTimestamp` as its timestamp while the deltas of the producer stay in the log
untouched. The vector `messageformat.v2.none.logappendtime` shows it: `FirstTimestamp` and `MaxTimestamp` are 19
milliseconds apart, the records still carry the deltas 0, 8 and 8, and `DumpLogSegments` prints the same
`LogAppendTime` for all three of them. With `CreateTime` `MaxTimestamp` is simply the largest timestamp of the
batch. An empty batch has `FirstTimestamp` -1 (`NO_TIMESTAMP`).

### Compression compresses the records, not a wrapper

There is no wrapper message any more. The 61 header bytes are always plain and the bytes after them are the
compressed block of the `RecordCount` records, so a reader knows how many records a batch holds, what their offsets
and timestamps are and whether the batch is transactional without decompressing anything. The codecs and their
framings are the ones of the message set — gzip, xerial-framed snappy, an LZ4 frame — with one difference:

* an **lz4** batch of the magic 2 always carries the *correct* frame descriptor checksum `0x82`. The KAFKA-3160
  breakage is bound to the message format v0 (`useBrokenFlagDescriptorChecksum = magic == 0`), so it can never
  appear in a record batch; `messageformat.v2.lz4.createtime` starts with `04 22 4d 18 60 40 82`.

Re-compressing a block with another implementation gives different — equally valid — bytes, which is why
`RecordBatch` keeps the records part exactly as it read it and `toBuffer()` reproduces a captured batch byte for
byte.

### Control batches

A batch whose attributes have bit 5 set carries **markers of the transaction protocol** instead of data. A client
never hands its records to an application: `MemoryRecords::getRecords()` drops them, and a `read_committed` consumer
uses them to decide which of the batches around them belong to a committed transaction. The only markers of Kafka
0.11 are the two ends of a transaction, written by the transaction coordinator itself:

```
Key => Version Type
  Version => int16 (0)
  Type    => int16 (0 ABORT, 1 COMMIT)

Value => Version CoordinatorEpoch
  Version          => int16 (0)
  CoordinatorEpoch => int32
```

Such a batch holds exactly one record, is never compressed, is always transactional, carries the `ProducerId` and
the `ProducerEpoch` of the transaction it ends and has `BaseSequence` -1 — the marker is not part of the sequence of
the producer. `messageformat.v2.none.control` is the marker that the coordinator of this container wrote when the
transaction of `messageformat.v2.none.transactional` was committed.

### What the broker stores, and what it answers

The message format of the **log** is the `message.format.version` of the topic (`log.message.format.version` of the
broker by default, `0.11.0` on the container of this branch). The broker converts a produced batch **down on write**
and converts the log **down on read** for every client that asks with a Fetch version that does not know the format
it holds. All of the following was measured against the 0.11.0.3 container by producing the very same record batch
v2 with a raw Produce v3 frame into three topics and reading each of them back with a raw Fetch v4, v3 and v1:

| `message.format.version` | Log stores | Fetch v0/v1 answers | Fetch v2/v3 answers | Fetch v4/v5 answers |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `0.9.0` | **v0**, timestamps dropped | v0 | v0 | v0 |
| `0.10.x` | **v1**, timestamps kept | **v0** | v1 | v1 |
| `0.11.0` (default) | v2, as produced | **v0** | **v1** | v2 |

A batch converted down becomes one *message per record* — the batch is unwrapped, not re-wrapped into a compressed
message — and a v0 answer loses every timestamp. A **`LogAppendTime`** batch is rebuilt with a magic 1 builder
(`AbstractRecords.convertRecordBatch()`), which stamps the append time and bit 3 of the attributes on **every** one
of those messages, where a 0.10.2.2 broker set the bit on the wrapper of a compressed set alone; measured on the
topic `t2-rb-lat`, whose three messages come back from a Fetch v3 with the attributes `0x08` and the very same
timestamp. `messageformat.v2.downconverted.v1` and
`messageformat.v2.downconverted.v0` are the two conversions of the log of `messageformat.v2.none.createtime`; the
raw frames that produced them are

```
00 01 00 03 00 00 00 0c 00 0a 74 32 2d 76 65 63 74 6f 72 73 ff ff ff ff 00 00 03 e8 00 00 00 00 00 10 00 00
00 00 00 01 00 0a 74 32 2d 72 62 2d 6e 6f 6e 65 00 00 00 01 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 10 00 00
```

for the Fetch **v3** and

```
00 01 00 01 00 00 00 0d 00 0a 74 32 2d 76 65 63 74 6f 72 73 ff ff ff ff 00 00 03 e8 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 01
00 0a 74 32 2d 72 62 2d 6e 6f 6e 65 00 00 00 01 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 10 00 00
```

for the Fetch **v1** (the client id is `t2-vectors`, the topic `t2-rb-none`, `max_bytes` 1 MiB, `max_wait_time` 1000
ms). Both are the request frames without their `Size` field, i.e. what `RawRecordBatchProbe` sends.

A client of this line therefore sees a record batch — and with it the headers, the producer id and the transaction
flags — only through a Fetch request of version 4 or above; everything below it is answered in a format that has no
place for any of them.

## Metadata API (key 3, v0 to v4)

This is the only request that may be addressed to any broker of the cluster. A 0.11.0.3 broker serves five
versions of it; the request has three shapes and the answer four.

```
Metadata Request (Version: 0) => [TopicName]
  TopicName => string

Metadata Request (Version: 1, 2 and 3) => [TopicName]     # the array is NULLABLE
  TopicName => string

Metadata Request (Version: 4) => [TopicName] AllowAutoTopicCreation
  TopicName              => string           # the array is NULLABLE
  AllowAutoTopicCreation => boolean          # since version 4
```

Version 1 (Kafka 0.10.0) made the topic array **nullable**, and the versions 2 (Kafka 0.10.1) and 3 (Kafka 0.11)
send the very same frame — `METADATA_REQUEST_V2 = METADATA_REQUEST_V1` and `METADATA_REQUEST_V3 =
METADATA_REQUEST_V2` in `Protocol.java` @ 0.11.0.3, only the answer grew each time. The nullable array is the whole
point of version 1: it separates two intentions that version 0 has to express with the same empty array.

| Version | `topics`         | Frame         | The broker answers                                                |
|---------|------------------|---------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 0       | `[]`             | `00 00 00 00` | every topic of the cluster                                        |
| 0       | `["a"]`          | one string    | topic `a`, creating it when `auto.create.topics.enable` is on     |
| 1 … 4   | `null`           | `ff ff ff ff` | every topic of the cluster, the internal ones included            |
| 1 … 4   | `[]`             | `00 00 00 00` | **no topic at all** — the brokers, the ids and an empty topic array |
| 1 … 3   | `["a"]`          | one string    | topic `a`, creating it when `auto.create.topics.enable` is on     |
| 4       | `["a"]`          | one string    | topic `a`; whether it is created is now the client's decision, see below |

`KafkaApis.handleTopicMetadataRequest` @ 0.11.0.3 spells the difference out — *"Handle old metadata request logic.
Version 0 has no way to specify 'no topics'"* — and the empty array of version 1 creates nothing either, because
only the topics a request NAMES are auto-created. An empty array is therefore the cheapest way of asking a broker
who the members of the cluster are (`metadata.request.v2.no-topics`), and it is what
[`AdminClient::findAllBrokers()`](../../src/Kafka/Admin/AdminClient.php) sends.

### `AllowAutoTopicCreation` (v4, Kafka 0.11, KIP-4): asking without creating

Until version 4 a metadata request for a topic that does not exist **created** it whenever the broker ran with the
default `auto.create.topics.enable=true` — with the broker's default partition count and replication factor, and
answered with the topic error code 5 (`LeaderNotAvailable`) and an empty partition list, because the controller had
not elected the leaders yet. There was no way for a client to say "just tell me whether it is there". Version 4
adds the trailing `allow_auto_topic_creation` BOOLEAN, and `KafkaApis.handleTopicMetadataRequest` @ 0.11.0.3
auto-creates a named topic only when

```scala
allowAutoCreation = config.autoCreateTopicsEnable && metadataRequest.allowAutoTopicCreation
```

holds. A request below version 4 has no such field, and `MetadataRequest.allowAutoTopicCreation()` answers `true`
for it, so nothing about the older versions changed.

This client makes the decision per caller, and the split is deliberate:

* **`Common\Cluster`** — the metadata path of a producer and a consumer — sends **`true`**. A named topic that does
  not exist yet has always been created for them, and version 4 must not silently change that behaviour.
* **`Admin\AdminClient`** — `describeTopics()`, `listTopics()` and `findAllBrokers()` — sends **`false`**. An
  administrator must be able to look at a topic without bringing it into being.

Verified on the container: a version 4 request naming an absent topic with the flag `false` is answered with the
topic error code **3** (`UnknownTopicOrPartition`) and an empty partition list, and `kafka-topics.sh --list`
afterwards does not show the topic (`metadata.request.v4.no-auto-creation` / `metadata.response.v4.unknown-topic`).

```
Metadata Response (Version: 0) => [Broker][TopicMetadata]
  Broker => NodeId Host Port
    NodeId => int32
    Host   => string
    Port   => int32
  TopicMetadata => TopicErrorCode TopicName [PartitionMetadata]
    TopicErrorCode => int16
    TopicName      => string
  PartitionMetadata => PartitionErrorCode PartitionId Leader Replicas Isr
    PartitionErrorCode => int16
    PartitionId        => int32
    Leader             => int32
    Replicas           => [int32]
    Isr                => [int32]

Metadata Response (Version: 1) => [Broker] ControllerId [TopicMetadata]
  Broker => NodeId Host Port Rack
    Rack => nullable string
  ControllerId => int32
  TopicMetadata => TopicErrorCode TopicName IsInternal [PartitionMetadata]
    IsInternal => boolean

Metadata Response (Version: 2) => [Broker] ClusterId ControllerId [TopicMetadata]
  ClusterId => nullable string

Metadata Response (Version: 3 and 4) => ThrottleTimeMs [Broker] ClusterId ControllerId [TopicMetadata]
  ThrottleTimeMs => int32          # since version 3
```

Version 3 (Kafka 0.11, KIP-124) put the `ThrottleTimeMs` in front of everything and changed nothing else, and
version 4 answers the very same frame — `METADATA_RESPONSE_V4 = METADATA_RESPONSE_V3` in `Protocol.java` @ 0.11.0.3,
because what version 4 added lives in the request. `MetadataResponseV3` therefore decodes the same bytes as
`MetadataResponse`; the class of the answer still has to match the version of the request, because a v2 answer read
with the v3 class would take the broker count for a throttle time.

`Leader` is `-1` while a leader election is in progress. The three fields that Kafka 0.10 added are:

* **`Rack`** (v1) — the `broker.rack` of that broker, a `NULLABLE_STRING`. The container of `docker-compose.yml`
  declares none, so it answers `ff ff` for its single broker, as does every cluster that is not rack aware.
* **`IsInternal`** (v1) — a `BOOLEAN` (one byte, `00` or `01`) for every topic. `Topic.isInternal` @ 0.10.2.2 knows
  exactly one internal topic, `__consumer_offsets`, the log the group coordinator keeps the committed offsets in;
  the vector `metadata.response.v2.internal-topic` is its metadata, with the five partitions of
  `offsets.topic.num.partitions=5`.
* **`ControllerId`** (v1) and **`ClusterId`** (v2) — see below. Note the ORDER: version 2 inserts the cluster id
  **before** the controller id, so a v1 answer and a v2 answer only differ in those bytes, and reading one with the
  class of the other silently walks off into the topic array.

Version 0 differs in one more thing that the grammar does not show. `errorUnavailableEndpoints = requestVersion == 0`
in `KafkaApis` @ 0.10.2.2: a version 0 answer reports the error code 9 (`ReplicaNotAvailable`) for a partition whose
replica set contains a broker that is currently down, while version 1 and above simply leave that broker out of the
`Replicas` and `Isr` lists. A client of v1+ therefore sees fewer errors and shorter replica lists for the same
cluster.

### `ControllerId`: which broker is the controller

`ControllerId` is the broker id of the active controller as the ANSWERING broker knows it, taken from its metadata
cache (`metadataCache.getControllerId.getOrElse(MetadataResponse.NO_CONTROLLER_ID)`); it is `-1` while the cluster
has no controller, i.e. while it is electing one. It is what the topic administration apis need, because
CreateTopics and DeleteTopics are served by the controller alone and answer 41 (`NotController`) on every other
broker.

On the container the field answers `0`, which is the `brokerid` of the `/controller` znode in ZooKeeper:

```
$ docker exec kafka-0-10-2-2 /opt/kafka/bin/zookeeper-shell.sh localhost:2181 get /controller
{"version":1,"brokerid":0,"timestamp":"1788928975914"}
```

Before Kafka 0.10.0 there was no way to ask this over the protocol at all: a client had to send a request that only
the controller serves and watch for the 41. [`AdminClient::findController()`](../../src/Kafka/Admin/AdminClient.php)
now reads the field instead, and asks for the metadata once more when it comes back `-1` or names a broker that is
not among the alive brokers of the same answer.

### `ClusterId`: the identity of the cluster

`ClusterId` arrived with version 2 (Kafka 0.10.1). The first broker of a cluster generates it —
`KafkaServer.getOrGenerateClusterId` @ 0.10.2.2, a 22 character URL-safe base64 encoding of a random UUID — and
stores it in ZooKeeper under `/cluster/id`, from where every other broker reads it, so every broker of one cluster
answers the same string. The container answers a real one, which is the content of that znode:

```
$ docker exec kafka-0-10-2-2 /opt/kafka/bin/zookeeper-shell.sh localhost:2181 get /cluster/id
{"version":"1","id":"_w793L5LQk2vaX_a6Lm3lw"}
```

The field is a `NULLABLE_STRING` because a broker may have none — a 0.10.1 broker that was upgraded from an older
release before it wrote the znode — so `null` is a legal answer and not an error.

## Produce API (key 0, v0 to v3)

```
ProduceRequest v0, v1 and v2 => RequiredAcks Timeout [TopicName [Partition MessageSetSize MessageSet]]
  RequiredAcks   => int16
  Timeout        => int32
  TopicName      => string
  Partition      => int32
  MessageSetSize => int32
```

```
ProduceRequest v3 => TransactionalId RequiredAcks Timeout [TopicName [Partition RecordSetSize RecordSet]]
  TransactionalId => nullable string
```

```
ProduceResponse v0 => [TopicName [Partition ErrorCode Offset]]
  TopicName => string
  Partition => int32
  ErrorCode => int16
  Offset    => int64
```

```
ProduceResponse v1 => [TopicName [Partition ErrorCode Offset]] ThrottleTime
  ThrottleTime => int32
```

```
ProduceResponse v2 and v3 => [TopicName [Partition ErrorCode Offset LogAppendTime]] ThrottleTime
  LogAppendTime => int64
```

`RequiredAcks`: `0` — do not wait and **send no response at all**; `1` — wait for the leader's local log; `-1` — wait
for all in-sync replicas.

**The request did not change between the versions 0 and 2.** `PRODUCE_REQUEST_V2` is `PRODUCE_REQUEST_V1` is
`PRODUCE_REQUEST_V0` in `clients/src/main/java/org/apache/kafka/common/protocol/Protocol.java` @ 0.11.0.3, so up to
version 2 a version only selects the layout of the answer — and, from 0.10.0 on, states which message format the
client speaks. Version 1 (Kafka 0.9) appended `ThrottleTime` to the **end** of the response, the milliseconds the
broker delayed this request because the client exceeded its `producer_byte_rate` quota (see "Quotas and throttle
time" below). Version 2 (Kafka 0.10.0) added `LogAppendTime` to every partition entry, **between** `Offset` and that
throttle time.

**Version 3 (Kafka 0.11.0, KIP-98) is the first version that changed the request.** It prefixes the body with the
nullable `TransactionalId` of the producer, and the record set of every partition is a **record batch of the message
format v2** (see "RecordBatch (message format v2)" above) instead of a message set. That batch is what carries the
record headers, the producer id, the producer epoch and the sequence numbers of an idempotent or transactional
producer, so **none of them can travel below version 3**: a client that writes a message set of the format v0 or v1
sends it as a Produce v2 request, which is what `ProducerConfig::MESSAGE_FORMAT_VERSION` selects here.

**The answer of version 3 is the answer of version 2, byte for byte.** `PRODUCE_RESPONSE_V3` **is**
`PRODUCE_RESPONSE_V2` in `Protocol.java` @ 0.11.0.3, and the 0.11.0.3 container really answers a version 3 request
with that frame — the vector `produce.response.v3` below is 50 bytes and ends with the `ThrottleTime` right behind
the `LogAppendTime`. The `log_start_offset` that a Produce answer eventually got is **Kafka 1.0** (Produce v5) and
is not part of this line. The four versions are therefore four classes — `ProduceResponse` (v3),
`ProduceResponseV2`, `ProduceResponseV1` and `ProduceResponseV0`, the first two of which read the same bytes and
differ only in the version of the request they belong to — with `ProduceResponsePartition` and
`ProduceResponsePartitionV0` for their partition entries.

`LogAppendTime` is the timestamp the broker stamped the whole appended batch with, in milliseconds since the epoch,
or **-1** when it stamped nothing. It is not -1 only for a topic configured with
`message.timestamp.type=LogAppendTime`: the broker then overwrites the timestamp of every record of the batch with
its own clock and reports that one value here (`Log.append` @ 0.11.0.3). In the message format v2 that overwriting
is a single field — the `MaxTimestamp` of the batch header plus bit 3 of its attributes, the records keep their
deltas — see "Offsets, timestamps and sequence numbers are deltas" above. `RecordMetadata::$timestamp` of this
client carries whichever of the two applies, exactly as the Java `RecordMetadata` does; the vectors
`produce.response.v3` and `produce.response.v3.log-append-time` below are the two answers.

**A Produce v3 request accepts the message format v2 and nothing else, and the check costs the connection.**
`ProduceRequest.parse` @ 0.11.0.3 walks the record set of every partition and throws
`InvalidRecordException: Produce requests with version 3 are only allowed to contain record batches with magic
version 2`, which the network layer turns into an `InvalidRequestException` and answers by **closing the socket** —
there is no error code for it. The check runs one way only; measured on the container with the very same records in
each of the three formats:

| Request | magic 0 | magic 1 | magic 2 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Produce v0, v1, v2 | accepted | accepted | accepted |
| Produce v3 | **connection closed** | **connection closed** | accepted |

A client that writes a message set of the format v0 or v1 therefore has to send a **Produce v2** request, which is
what `message.format.version` selects here. Below version 3 the broker still does not care: it stores whatever it is
given in the `message.format.version` of the topic and converts it on append (see "What the broker stores, and what
it answers" above), so a magic 0 batch sent with a Produce v2 lands in the log as a record batch v2 without a
timestamp, and a magic 2 batch sent with a Produce v0 lands there unchanged.

**A transactional id needs the message format v2.** It travels in the version 3 request alone, so a transaction can
only ever be written in the message format v2; `Client::produceRecords()` refuses a transactional id together with a
`message.format.version` below `0.11.0` before anything is sent.

## The idempotent producer

Kafka 0.11 gave the producer a delivery guarantee that no release before it had: **exactly once, in order, within
one producer session** (KIP-98). It costs three fields of the record batch v2 and one new api, and the whole of it
happens between the producer and the *leader* of a partition — no coordinator is involved, and a producer that only
wants idempotence never touches `__transaction_state`.

**What the producer sends.** Every batch of an idempotent producer carries

* the **producer id** that `InitProducerId` handed out (`ProducerId`, int64),
* the **epoch** of that id (`ProducerEpoch`, int16, always 0 without a transactional id),
* the **base sequence** of its first record (`BaseSequence`, int32), the last one being
  `BaseSequence + LastOffsetDelta`,

and the sequence numbers are **per topic-partition**, counted from 0, gapless, and *re-used unchanged* when a batch
has to be sent again. A batch that carries `-1` as its producer id is not deduplicated at all, which is what a
plain producer writes.

**What the broker keeps.** `ProducerStateManager` @ 0.11.0.3 holds, per **log** (i.e. per topic-partition) and per
producer id, exactly **one** `ProducerIdEntry`: the epoch, the first and the last sequence number of the batch it
appended last, that batch's offset and timestamp. That is the whole memory of a 0.11 broker — *the batch before the
last one is already forgotten* — and it is why the Java producer forces `max.in.flight.requests.per.connection = 1`
when idempotence is on: a second request in flight can reorder two batches of a partition, and the reordered one is
no longer the batch the broker remembers. (The window of five batches per producer id that later releases keep,
`ProducerStateEntry.NumBatchesToRetain`, is Kafka 1.0 and later, not 0.11.) This client is synchronous — one
produce request is written and its answer read before the next one — so it satisfies the requirement by
construction.

**What the broker checks**, in this order (`ProducerStateManager.validateAppend`, `Log.analyzeAndValidateProducerState`):

1. the batch is *exactly* the one the entry holds — same epoch, same first and last sequence: the request is a
   **duplicate**, and the broker answers it with the error code **0 and the base offset, the timestamp and the
   `LogAppendTime` of the original append**, without writing anything;
2. the epoch of the batch is **below** the one of the entry: **47** `InvalidProducerEpoch` — another producer took
   this producer id over;
3. the epoch is **above** it: the entry is reset, and the first sequence of the batch has to be **0**, otherwise
   **45**;
4. the first sequence is not `lastSeq + 1` (with a wrap-around from `Int.MaxValue` to 0): **45**
   `OutOfOrderSequenceNumber`.

Verified against the container, one partition, one producer id, sequences as the table says:

| Batch sent                                       | Answer                                        |
|--------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------|
| sequences 0-1, first time                        | 0, `baseOffset = 1`, `LogAppendTime = -1`     |
| the very same bytes again                        | **0**, `baseOffset = 1`, `LogAppendTime = 1788958757854` |
| sequence 2                                       | 0, `baseOffset = 3`                           |
| sequences 0-1 again, now that 2 was appended     | **45** — the older batch is no longer remembered |
| sequence 9 (a gap)                               | **45**                                        |
| sequence 3, after the 45                         | 0, `baseOffset = 4` — a rejected batch changes nothing |
| sequence 0 of another *partition*, same producer | 0 — the sequence space is per partition       |
| sequence 5 as the *first* batch of a new producer id | **45** — a new producer id has to start at 0 |
| epoch 1, sequences 0-1, then epoch 0             | 0, 0, then **47** for every batch of epoch 0  |
| epoch 2 with a first sequence of 5, then 0       | **45**, then 0 — a new epoch has to start at 0 |

**The error code 46 (`DuplicateSequenceNumber`) never reaches a client of a 0.11.0.3 broker.** Its own duplicate
check (step 1 above) runs in `Log.analyzeAndValidateProducerState`, guarded by `isFromClient`, and returns the
original offset *before* `validateAppend` is ever reached; the `DuplicateSequenceNumberException` that
`validateAppend` can throw is therefore only reachable on the replication path. A client sees the *success* answer
for a duplicate, and **45** for a duplicate of a batch that is no longer the last one. The `LogAppendTime` of that
success answer is the giveaway: a `CreateTime` topic answers `-1` for a real append and the stored timestamp of the
batch for a duplicate.

**What this client does** (`TransactionManager`, `Producer\Internals`): it holds the producer id, its epoch and
the next sequence of every topic-partition, asks for the producer id lazily with the first flush, stamps every
batch, and moves the sequence of a partition on by the number of records the broker acknowledged. On a **47** the
producer is finished: the state is fatal and every further send reports the same `ProducerFencedException`. On a
**45** an idempotent producer throws its producer id away and asks for a new one with the next batch — the batch
that hit the error is reported to the caller, and the deduplication starts over — which is what `Sender.failBatch`
@ 0.11.0.3 does; a *transactional* producer must not, because that would bump the epoch behind the back of an open
transaction. A **46**, should a broker ever send one, counts as an append: the sequence numbers are consumed and
the partition is reported as accepted with an unknown offset.

`enable.idempotence = true` also constrains two options, and this client refuses the configuration exactly as the
Java producer does: `acks` has to be `all` (`-1`) and `retries` must not be 0. Both are overridden when the caller
did not set them; `retries` becomes 3 here rather than the `Integer.MAX_VALUE` of the Java producer, which has a
background sender to bound a batch by time instead of by a number of attempts. The `acks` rule is enforced one
layer lower as well: `Client::produce()` refuses producer state next to anything but `acks = all`, because a
request the broker does not answer leaves the sequence numbers of this client where they were and would send every
following batch with the very same ones.

**The guarantee ends with the producer.** A new producer asks for a new producer id, so it cannot deduplicate
against anything the previous one wrote, and an application that re-sends a record itself builds a *new* batch,
which the broker has no way of recognising. Deduplication across sessions is what a `transactional.id` is for.

## Transactions

The second half of KIP-98, and the one the whole api block 24-28 exists for: several partitions - and the
committed offsets of a consumer group - are written **atomically**, so that a reader either sees all of it or none
of it. The idempotent producer above is the foundation (the producer id, the epoch and the sequence numbers stay
exactly what they were); a transaction adds a **coordinator**, a **flag on the record batch**, a **control batch**
per partition and one option on the reading side.

### The pieces

| Piece | What it is |
|---|---|
| `transactional.id` | The name the *application* gives its producer. It is what makes the guarantee survive a restart, and only one producer may use it at a time. |
| Transaction coordinator | The broker that leads the partition of the internal topic `__transaction_state` the id hashes to; found with the GroupCoordinator api and `CoordinatorType = 1`. It serves InitProducerId, AddPartitionsToTxn, AddOffsetsToTxn and EndTxn. |
| `__transaction_state` | The log of the coordinator: producer id, epoch, timeout, state (`Empty`, `Ongoing`, `PrepareCommit`, `PrepareAbort`, `CompleteCommit`, `CompleteAbort`, `Dead`) and the list of partitions of the open transaction. It is created by the **first** coordinator lookup of any transactional id. |
| The transactional bit | Bit 4 (0x10) of the record batch attributes. A batch that carries it belongs to a transaction; the `transactional_id` of the Produce v3 request names which. |
| The control batch | Bits 4 and 5 (0x30) and one record whose key is `(version, type)` and whose value is `(version, coordinatorEpoch)`: **COMMIT** (type 1) or **ABORT** (type 0). It is written by the coordinator, not by the producer, and no client ever hands it to an application. |
| Last stable offset (LSO) | The first offset of the oldest transaction that is still open, or the high watermark when none is. It is the bound of a `read_committed` fetch and what version 2 of the Offsets api answers such a reader for `LATEST`. |

### The flow of one transaction

```
producer                        transaction coordinator          partition leaders        group coordinator
   |  GroupCoordinator(type=1)  |                                       |                        |
   |--------------------------->|  (creates __transaction_state)        |                        |
   |  InitProducerId(txn id)    |                                       |                        |
   |--------------------------->|  producer id + epoch+1, aborts        |                        |
   |                            |  whatever the last incarnation left   |                        |
   |  ---- beginTransaction() is a client-side state change, no frame ----                       |
   |  AddPartitionsToTxn        |                                       |                        |
   |--------------------------->|  state Ongoing, timeout starts        |                        |
   |  Produce v3 (txn id, batch attributes 0x10)  ------------------->  |                        |
   |  AddOffsetsToTxn(group)    |                                       |                        |
   |--------------------------->|  adds __consumer_offsets-N            |                        |
   |  TxnOffsetCommit(group)  ------------------------------------------------------------------>|
   |  EndTxn(commit|abort)      |                                       |                        |
   |--------------------------->|  PrepareCommit / PrepareAbort         |                        |
   |             <--------------|  WriteTxnMarkers ------------------>  |  control batch         |
   |                            |  CompleteCommit / CompleteAbort       |                        |
```

Two things about that picture are easy to get wrong and were verified against the container:

* **`EndTxn` is answered before the markers exist.** The coordinator answers as soon as it has written its decision
  into `__transaction_state`; the control batches follow, one `WriteTxnMarkers` per partition leader. A
  `read_committed` consumer therefore sees the records of a committed transaction a moment *after* `EndTxn`
  returned, which is why the integration tests of this line poll for them.
* **A transaction is started by `AddPartitionsToTxn`, not by anything the client calls "begin".** The protocol has
  no BeginTransaction request at all.

### What a reader sees

`isolation.level` is one byte in the Fetch (v4+) and the Offsets (v2) request, and it changes three things at once:

| | `read_uncommitted` (0) | `read_committed` (1) |
|---|---|---|
| The answer ends at | the high watermark | the **last stable offset** |
| `last_stable_offset` of the answer | **-1** | the real LSO |
| `aborted_transactions` of the answer | **null** | an array, possibly empty |
| Offsets v2 `LATEST` answers | the high watermark | the last stable offset |

**The broker does not filter anything.** A `read_committed` answer of a partition that holds an aborted
transaction carries the aborted records *and* the ABORT control batch, and only names the transaction in
`aborted_transactions` as a `(producer_id, first_offset)` pair. Verified on the container: a partition with one
aborted transaction of two records answers `hw = 3`, `lso = 3`, one aborted transaction, two record batches and
two records - both of which a client has to drop. The filtering algorithm is
`Fetcher.PartitionRecords.nextFetchedRecord()` @ 0.11.0.3 and this client implements it in
`Consumer\Internals\AbortedTransactionFilter`:

1. walk the **batches** of the answer in log order;
2. every aborted transaction whose `first_offset` is at or below the last offset of the current batch is taken off
   the list and its producer id is remembered as aborted;
3. a control batch never reaches an application, and an **ABORT** marker makes its producer id "not aborted" again;
4. every other batch of an aborted producer id is skipped whole.

A control batch is dropped in **both** isolation levels - `read_uncommitted` shows the aborted records but never
the marker.

The level travels in the **Offsets** request as well, and this client sends it there for the same reason the Java
consumer does: a `read_committed` consumer whose `endOffsets()` answered the high watermark would wait for records
it is never going to be shown. `Client::fetchPartitions()` and `Client::fetchTopicPartitionOffsetsForTimes()` -
