`Data\CreateTopicsRequestConfig` therefore declares the field as `TYPE_NULLABLE_STRING` — the wire truth — while
this client never sends a null: a non-null value is byte for byte identical under either type, so no captured vector
changes.

**Only the active controller serves this api.** `KafkaApis.handleCreateTopicsRequest` @ 0.11.0.3 answers every topic
of the request with the error code 41 (NotController) when `controller.isActive` is false, before it looks at the
request at all. Metadata v0 does not name the controller; `ControllerId` of Metadata v1 does, which is what
`Admin\AdminClient::findController()` reads - see the "Metadata API" section above.

A topic is described in **one of two ways**, and mixing them is an error:

* `NumPartitions` and `ReplicationFactor` are set and the assignment array is **empty**: the controller places the
  replicas with `AdminUtils.assignReplicasToBrokers`;
* both are `-1` (`CreateTopicsRequest.NO_NUM_PARTITIONS` / `NO_REPLICATION_FACTOR`) and the assignment array names
  the broker ids of every partition, the preferred leader first.

Setting both is answered with **42 (InvalidRequest)** and the message *"Both numPartitions or replicationFactor and
replicasAssignments were set. Both cannot be used at the same time."*, which is the check at the top of
`AdminManager.createTopics`. The broker deliberately does **not** verify that an explicit assignment names brokers
that exist ("we don't check that replicaAssignment contains unknown brokers - unlike in add-partitions case, this
follows the existing logic in TopicCommand"): a topic assigned to a broker id that no broker has is written to
ZooKeeper, never gets a leader, and the request answers **7 (RequestTimedOut)** after `Timeout` - the topic then
exists in ZooKeeper but never appears in the metadata of the cluster, and DeleteTopics answers 3 for it.

`Timeout` is how long the **controller** waits for the topic to be created on it before it answers, not a socket
timeout. `Values <= 0 will trigger topic creation and return immediately`: the answer arrives at once and reports
**7 (RequestTimedOut)** for every topic that was accepted, although the creation is under way and finishes shortly
afterwards (observed: the topic is in the metadata of the broker ~50 ms later). The same is true for a timeout that
is simply too short. A successful answer means the topic exists **on the controller**; the other brokers learn about
it with the next metadata update, so a Metadata request may still answer 5 (LeaderNotAvailable) for a moment.

`ValidateOnly` runs the whole validation - the topic name, the replica placement, every topic-level option - and
answers without writing anything to ZooKeeper (`AdminUtils.validateCreateOrUpdateTopic` instead of
`createOrUpdateTopicPartitionAssignmentPathInZK`). A validated topic comes back with the error code **0** and does
not exist afterwards. Version 0 has no such flag, and the Java client refuses to even build a version 0 request with
it set.

Error codes observed on the 0.10.2.2 container, with the `ErrorMessage` that version 1 carries:

| Code | Name                     | Request that produces it                          | ErrorMessage                                                                 |
|------|--------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 0    | None                     | The topic was created (or validated)              | `null`                                                                       |
| 7    | RequestTimedOut          | `Timeout` of 0, or an assignment to an unknown broker | `null`                                                                   |
| 17   | InvalidTopicException    | A topic name outside `[a-zA-Z0-9._-]`             | `topic name ... is illegal, contains a character other than ASCII alphanumerics, '.', '_' and '-'` |
| 36   | TopicAlreadyExists       | A topic of that name exists                       | `Topic 'x' already exists.`                                                  |
| 37   | InvalidPartitions        | `NumPartitions` = 0                               | `number of partitions must be larger than 0`                                 |
| 38   | InvalidReplicationFactor | `ReplicationFactor` larger than the broker count  | `replication factor: 2 larger than available brokers: 1`                     |
| 40   | InvalidConfig            | An unknown topic-level option **name**            | `Unknown Log configuration nope.ms.`                                         |
| 41   | NotController            | Sent to a broker that is not the controller       | `null`                                                                       |
| 42   | InvalidRequest           | Partitions/factor **and** an explicit assignment; the same topic twice in one request | see above          |
| 44   | PolicyViolation          | A `create.topic.policy.class.name` refused it     | the message of the policy                                                    |

**Quirk: an unparsable option VALUE is not 40.** `LogConfig.validate` first calls `validateNames`, which throws
Kafka's own `InvalidConfigurationException` - an `ApiException`, mapped to 40 - for an unknown option name, and then
`configDef.parse`, which throws the plain `ConfigException` of the common config framework. That one is not an
`ApiException`, so `Errors.forException` falls through to **-1 (Unknown)**: `retention.ms=soon` is answered with the
code -1 and the message `Invalid value soon for configuration retention.ms: Not a number of type LONG`. Only the
name of an option is reported as 40.

**Quirk: duplicates are handled by the broker, not by the schema.** The wire format is an array, so the same topic
can appear twice in one request; `CreateTopicsRequest.duplicateTopics` collects those and `KafkaApis` answers each
of them with 42 (InvalidRequest) and a message that names them. This client keys its topic map by name and therefore
cannot produce such a frame.

## DeleteTopics API (key 20, v0 and v1)

Deletes topics through the protocol. The api arrived with Kafka **0.10.1**; a 0.10.2.2 broker serves **v0** only.

```
DeleteTopicsRequest => [Topic] Timeout
  Topic   => string
  Timeout => int32
```

```
DeleteTopicsResponse (v0) => [TopicErrorCode]
  TopicErrorCode => Topic ErrorCode
    Topic     => string
    ErrorCode => int16

DeleteTopicsResponse (v1) => ThrottleTimeMs [TopicErrorCode]
  ThrottleTimeMs => int32          # since version 1
```

The request is a plain array of topic **names**, not of structures, and the answer carries the bare
`TOPIC_ERROR_CODE` entries - there is no `ErrorMessage` in this api at all, the reason of a failure is only in the
log of the broker. Version 1 (Kafka 0.11, KIP-124) changed the request in no way
(`DELETE_TOPICS_REQUEST_V1 = DELETE_TOPICS_REQUEST_V0` in `Protocol.java` @ 0.11.0.3) and prefixed the answer with
the throttle time.

**Only the active controller serves it**, exactly like CreateTopics: `KafkaApis.handleDeleteTopicsRequest` @
0.11.0.3 answers every topic of the request with 41 (NotController) when `controller.isActive` is false.

**Quirk: an empty topic array is answered by every broker with an empty array.** The handler builds the answer by
mapping over the topics of the **request** (`deleteTopicRequest.topics.asScala.map { topic => (topic,
Errors.NOT_CONTROLLER) }`), so a request without topics produces an answer without entries - on the controller and
on every follower alike. An empty DeleteTopics is therefore useless as a **controller probe**, which is how a
client of a line below 0.10 has to find the controller at all: it has to name **one topic whose name can never be
legal** (`#kafka-client-controller-probe#` - `Topic.validate` only allows `[a-zA-Z0-9._-]`), so that the controller
cannot find it in its metadata cache and answers 3 (UnknownTopicOrPartition) without touching ZooKeeper while every
other broker answers 41 for it, and nothing can ever be deleted by the probe. Verified against the container: the
empty request `00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00` comes back as `00 00 00 00`, the named one as one entry with the code 3.
From Kafka 0.10.0 on none of that is needed - `Admin\AdminClient::findController()` reads the `ControllerId` of a
Metadata answer instead.

**Deletion is asynchronous.** `AdminUtils.deleteTopic` only writes the topic into `/admin/delete_topics` in
ZooKeeper; the controller then stops the replicas and removes the partitions. `Timeout` is how long the controller
waits for that before it answers, and `Values <= 0 will trigger topic deletion and return immediately` - the answer
is then **7 (RequestTimedOut)** for every topic whose deletion was started. Measured on the single-broker container
with `delete.topic.enable=true`:

* `Timeout = 30000`: the controller answered **0** after ~60 ms, and the topic was gone from a Metadata answer ~50 ms
  after that;
* `Timeout = 0`: the answer came back at once with **7**, and the topic was gone from Metadata ~50 ms later.

So even a successful answer only says that the controller is done; a client that waits for the topic to disappear
has to poll the metadata. A cluster whose brokers run with `delete.topic.enable=false` - the **default** of Kafka
0.10 - accepts the request the same way and never carries the deletion out.

| Code | Name                     | Request that produces it                                            |
|------|--------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 0    | None                     | The topic was deleted within `Timeout`                              |
| 3    | UnknownTopicOrPartition  | The metadata cache of the broker has no such topic - also the answer to deleting the same topic a second time, once the first deletion finished |
| 7    | RequestTimedOut          | `Timeout` of 0, or a deletion that was not finished in time         |
| 29   | TopicAuthorizationFailed | The client may describe the topic but not delete it                 |
| 41   | NotController            | Sent to a broker that is not the controller                         |

A topic that is **already marked** for deletion is accepted again without an error: `AdminManager.deleteTopics`
swallows the `TopicAlreadyMarkedForDeletionException` "and still track[s] deletion allowing multiple calls to wait
for deletion".

## OffsetForLeaderEpoch API (key 23, v0)

The api of **KIP-101**, "Alter Replication Protocol to use Leader Epoch rather than High Watermark for Truncation",
new in Kafka 0.11. It is a **broker-to-broker** request and this client sends it nowhere; the classes and the
vectors below exist because the api is part of the protocol of 0.11.0.3 and this document describes all of it.

```
OffsetForLeaderEpochRequest (v0) => [Topic [PartitionId LeaderEpoch]]
  Topic       => string
  PartitionId => int32
  LeaderEpoch => int32
```

```
OffsetForLeaderEpochResponse (v0) => [Topic [ErrorCode PartitionId EndOffset]]
  Topic       => string
  ErrorCode   => int16
  PartitionId => int32
  EndOffset   => int64
```

Note the order of a partition entry of the answer: the **error code comes first**, before the partition id, which no
other api of this protocol does.

The question it asks is *"you were the leader of the epoch `e` of this partition — which offset does that epoch end
at?"*. Until 0.11 a follower that came back after a leader change truncated its log to the high watermark it
remembered, which can diverge from the new leader in the two scenarios KIP-101 describes; from 0.11 on it asks the
new leader instead and truncates to the answer. The epoch a follower asks with is the `PartitionLeaderEpoch` that
the record batches on its disk carry, which is the other half of KIP-101 — see the "RecordBatch (message format v2)"
section.

`Log.endOffsetForEpoch` @ 0.11.0.3 answers the **start offset of the first epoch above the requested one**, and
`EpochEndOffset.UNDEFINED_EPOCH_OFFSET` (`-1`) when its epoch cache cannot answer at all — a log written by a broker
below 0.11, or an epoch it has never heard of. For the epoch the leader is **still** leading the answer is the log
end offset, which is what the vector `offsetforleaderepoch.response.v0` shows: partition 0 of `t3-throttle` holds
two records, has only ever been led by broker 0, and the epoch 0 is answered with the end offset 2. Three answers
of the container, all of them with the error code **0**:

| Partition                     | Requested epoch | `end_offset`                     |
|-------------------------------|-----------------|----------------------------------|
| two records, led by broker 0  | 0               | 2, the log end offset            |
| two records, led by broker 0  | 1               | `-1`, an epoch it never had      |
| **empty**, led by broker 0    | 0               | `-1`                             |

The last row is worth remembering: **an empty partition answers `-1` for the epoch it is being led with**, because
the leader-epoch cache of a log is only written when the first record is appended. The code 0 with the offset `-1`
is therefore "I have no entry for that epoch", not a failure — and a follower that gets it truncates to nothing.

There is **no throttle time and no top-level error code** in either frame: KIP-124 landed in the same release but
left this api alone, because a client quota does not apply to replication traffic. Every partition carries its own
code, and a 0.11.0.3 broker reports 0, 3 (`UnknownTopicOrPartition`, verified with the vector
`offsetforleaderepoch.response.v0.unknown-partition`, which answers the code 3 with the end offset `-1`), 6
(`NotLeaderForPartition`) and 31 (`ClusterAuthorizationFailed`).

**A broker without an authorizer answers a plain client just as it answers a follower.**
`KafkaApis.handleOffsetForLeaderEpochRequest` @ 0.11.0.3 calls `authorizeClusterAction(request)`, which passes
unconditionally when no `authorizer.class.name` is configured, so the vectors above were captured from an ordinary
client connection on the container. On a secured cluster the same request is answered with the code 31.
## DeleteRecords API (key 21, v0)

Deletes the records **before an offset** of a partition. The api arrived with Kafka **0.11** (KIP-107); a 0.11.0.3
broker serves **v0** only.

```
DeleteRecordsRequest => [Topic] Timeout
  Topic => TopicName [Partition]
    TopicName => string
    Partition => PartitionId Offset
      PartitionId => int32
      Offset      => int64
  Timeout => int32
```

```
DeleteRecordsResponse => ThrottleTimeMs [Topic]
  ThrottleTimeMs => int32
  Topic => TopicName [Partition]
    TopicName => string
    Partition => PartitionId LowWatermark ErrorCode
      PartitionId  => int32
      LowWatermark => int64
      ErrorCode    => int16
```

Until Kafka 0.11 the only ways to get rid of records were the retention of the topic - by time or by size - and
deleting the topic itself. This api moves the **low watermark** of a partition (`logStartOffset` in the broker,
`log_start_offset` in a Fetch v5 answer) forward: everything **below** `Offset` becomes unreadable at once, the
record *at* `Offset` stays, and the segments that are then completely below the watermark are removed by the log
cleaner afterwards. `Offset = -1` (`DeleteRecordsRequest.HIGH_WATERMARK`) means "up to the high watermark", i.e.
everything that is fully replicated.

The answer reports the new low watermark of every partition, and it is the same number that three other places of
the protocol report: an Offsets request with `EARLIEST`, the `log_start_offset` of a Fetch v5 answer, and the
`LowWatermark` of the next DeleteRecords. Verified on the container with a partition holding five records, deleting
before the offset 2:

* `DeleteRecords(offset = 2)` → error 0, `LowWatermark = 2`;
* `Offsets(EARLIEST)` → 2, `Offsets(LATEST)` → 5, so nothing else moved;
* a raw **Fetch v5** at the offset 2 → `high_watermark = 5`, `log_start_offset = 2`, and the records come back;
* the same Fetch at the offset 0 or 1 → error **1 (OffsetOutOfRange)** with `high_watermark = -1` and
  `log_start_offset = -1`, which is what a consumer that was reading below the new watermark now sees.

**The leader of each partition serves it**, exactly like Produce and Fetch: `KafkaApis.handleDeleteRecordsRequest`
hands the partitions to `ReplicaManager.deleteRecords`, which works on the local `Partition` and answers
6 (NotLeaderForPartition) for a partition this broker does not lead. `Client::deleteRecords()` therefore splits the
request by leader like a produce, and `Admin\AdminClient::deleteRecords()` reports the new watermark per partition.

`Timeout` is how long the **leader** waits for the new watermark to be acknowledged by the followers of the ISR
(`DelayedDeleteRecords`) before it answers; the deletion itself is done on the leader before it waits, and nothing
is rolled back when the timeout expires - the partition is simply reported with 7 (RequestTimedOut). On the
one-broker container the ISR is the leader itself, so every answer is immediate.

Error codes observed on the 0.11.0.3 container:

| Code | Name                     | Request that produces it                                                     |
|------|--------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 0    | None                     | The records below the offset are gone; `LowWatermark` is the new start offset |
| 1    | OffsetOutOfRange         | An offset **above the high watermark**, and every negative offset except -1   |
| 3    | UnknownTopicOrPartition  | A topic that is not in the metadata cache of the broker, or a partition the topic does not have |
| 6    | NotLeaderForPartition    | Sent to a broker that does not lead the partition                            |
| 7    | RequestTimedOut          | The new watermark was not acknowledged by the ISR within `Timeout`           |
| 29   | TopicAuthorizationFailed | The client may describe the topic but not delete from it                     |

A partition that failed carries the low watermark **-1** (`DeleteRecordsResponse.INVALID_LOW_WATERMARK`), never a
real offset.

**Quirk: deleting below the current watermark is not an error.** A request for an offset that is at or below the low
watermark the partition already has answers 0 and the *current* watermark, and changes nothing:
`Log.maybeIncrementLogStartOffset` only moves the value forward. Repeating the same request is therefore harmless,
which is what makes the api usable as "make sure everything before X is gone".

**Quirk: a negative offset other than -1 is 1, not 42.** `ReplicaManager.deleteRecordsOnLocalLog` resolves -1 to the
high watermark first and then checks `convertedOffset < 0`, throwing an `OffsetOutOfRangeException` for everything
else - so `-2`, which the Offsets api reads as "earliest", is answered with the error code **1** here.

**Quirk: an unknown topic is answered per partition, and it is not created.** The answer holds one entry per
partition of the *request*, so a topic the cluster does not have comes back with 3 for each of its requested
partitions - and unlike a Metadata request that names an unknown topic, this one does **not** create it while
`auto.create.topics.enable` is on: `KafkaApis` only asks `metadataCache.contains(topic)`. Internal topics are
refused as well, by `Topic.isInternal` inside `ReplicaManager.deleteRecordsOnLocalLog`.

## DescribeConfigs API (key 32, v0)

Reads the configuration of a topic or of a broker. The api arrived with Kafka **0.11** (KIP-133); a 0.11.0.3 broker
serves **v0** only.

```
DescribeConfigsRequest => [Resource]
  Resource => ResourceType ResourceName [ConfigName]
    ResourceType => int8
    ResourceName => string
    ConfigName   => nullable array of string
```

```
DescribeConfigsResponse => ThrottleTimeMs [Resource]
  ThrottleTimeMs => int32
  Resource => ErrorCode ErrorMessage ResourceType ResourceName [ConfigEntry]
    ErrorCode    => int16
    ErrorMessage => nullable string
    ResourceType => int8
    ResourceName => string
    ConfigEntry  => ConfigName ConfigValue ReadOnly IsDefault IsSensitive
      ConfigName  => string
      ConfigValue => nullable string
      ReadOnly    => boolean
      IsDefault   => boolean
      IsSensitive => boolean
```

`ResourceType` is the id of `org.apache.kafka.common.requests.ResourceType` @ 0.11.0.3 - `UNKNOWN(0)`, `ANY(1)`,
**`TOPIC(2)`**, `GROUP(3)`, **`BROKER(4)`** - and only the two bold ones are accepted by this api; anything else is
answered with 42 and `Unexpected resource type GROUP for resource …`. Those numbers are **not** the ones of the ACL
`ResourceType` of the same release, and they are not the ones of the `ConfigResource.Type` enum that Kafka 1.0
introduced. `ResourceName` is the topic name, or the **broker id as a decimal string**.

`ConfigName` is a NULLABLE array and the two empty cases mean different things: `null` (`ff ff ff ff`) asks for
**every** option of the resource, an **empty** array asks for none and answers with an empty entry list.

**A topic resource is answered by any broker, a broker resource only by that broker.**
`AdminManager.describeConfigs` @ 0.11.0.3 reads the entity config of a topic out of ZooKeeper and merges it with the
log defaults of the broker that answers, so every broker can serve it; a broker resource is the live `KafkaConfig`
of the process, and the manager compares the requested id with its own `config.brokerId` and throws for any other
one. `Admin\AdminClient::describeConfigs()` therefore splits a call by resource: the broker resources go to the
node they name, everything else to any broker.

The three flags are computed by `AdminManager.describeConfigs`:

* `ReadOnly` is **true for every entry of a broker resource** - a 0.11 broker cannot change its own configuration at
  runtime, dynamic broker configs are Kafka 1.1 (KIP-226) - and false for every entry of a topic;
* `IsDefault` says that nothing was configured *for this resource*: for a topic, that the option is missing from its
  ZooKeeper node and the value shown is the one the broker-level default implies; for a broker, that the option is
  missing from its `server.properties`;
* `IsSensitive` is true for every option whose `ConfigDef.Type` is `PASSWORD`, and **the value of such an entry is
  always `null`** on the wire (`valueAsString = if (isSensitive) null else …`) - `ssl.keystore.password` of the
  container comes back as `ff ff` although the broker has one.

Error codes observed on the container:

| Code | Name                       | Request that produces it                                            | ErrorMessage                                          |
|------|----------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------|
| 0    | None                       | The configuration follows                                           | `null`                                                 |
| 17   | InvalidTopic               | A topic name outside `[a-zA-Z0-9._-]`                               | `Topic name "…" is illegal, it contains a character other than ASCII alphanumerics, '.', '_' and '-'` |
| 29   | TopicAuthorizationFailed   | The client may describe the topic but not its configuration         | `null`                                                 |
| 31   | ClusterAuthorizationFailed | The client may not read a broker resource                           | `null`                                                 |
| 42   | InvalidRequest             | A broker id that is not the one that answers                        | `Unexpected broker id, expected 0, but received 7`     |
| 42   | InvalidRequest             | A broker id that is not a number                                    | `Broker id must be an integer, but it is: nope`        |
| 42   | InvalidRequest             | A resource type other than 2 and 4                                  | `Unexpected resource type GROUP for resource t5-group` |

**Quirk: a topic that does not exist is answered with the DEFAULTS, not with an error.**
`AdminUtils.fetchEntityConfig` returns empty properties for a topic that has no ZooKeeper node, and the broker
merges those with its own log defaults - so describing `t5-no-such-topic` answers the error code **0** and the full
default configuration, with every entry flagged `IsDefault`. The api says nothing about whether a topic exists;
Metadata does. (It does not create the topic either.)

**Quirk: an option name the broker does not know is silently dropped.** `createResponseConfig` filters the values it
*has* by the requested names, so a request for `no.such.option` answers the error code 0 and an **empty** entry
list - there is no "unknown config" error in this api, unlike in AlterConfigs.

**Quirk: the answer is not in the order of the request.** The broker walks a Scala `Map`, both over the resources
and over the entries of one resource, so the two options of `describeconfigs.request.v0.broker` come back in the
opposite order. A client matches an entry by name and a resource by type and name, never by position.

## AlterConfigs API (key 33, v0)

Replaces the configuration of a topic. The api arrived with Kafka **0.11** (KIP-133); a 0.11.0.3 broker serves
**v0** only.

```
AlterConfigsRequest => [Resource] ValidateOnly
  Resource => ResourceType ResourceName [ConfigEntry]
    ResourceType => int8
    ResourceName => string
    ConfigEntry  => ConfigName ConfigValue
      ConfigName  => string
      ConfigValue => nullable string
  ValidateOnly => boolean
```

```
AlterConfigsResponse => ThrottleTimeMs [Resource]
  ThrottleTimeMs => int32
  Resource => ErrorCode ErrorMessage ResourceType ResourceName
    ErrorCode    => int16
    ErrorMessage => nullable string
    ResourceType => int8
    ResourceName => string
```

The counterpart of DescribeConfigs, and the api that replaces `kafka-configs.sh --alter`. The resource entry is the
one of DescribeConfigs with the configuration in place of the filter, the answer entry is the one of DescribeConfigs
without the configuration, and `ConfigEntry` is the shared `CONFIG_ENTRY` schema of `Protocol.java` - the same two
fields the `configs` array of a CreateTopics request carries, except that 0.11 declares the **value nullable** here.

**Any broker serves it.** `KafkaApis.handleAlterConfigsRequest` has no controller check at all: it hands the
resources to `AdminManager.alterConfigs`, which writes the topic node in ZooKeeper, and the brokers pick the change
up from there. That is the difference to CreateTopics and DeleteTopics, which only the active controller answers.

**The entries are the WHOLE configuration, not a patch.** `AdminManager.alterConfigs` builds a fresh `Properties`
from them and calls `AdminUtils.changeTopicConfig`, which **replaces** the ZooKeeper node of the topic. Verified on
the container: after setting `retention.ms` and `cleanup.policy` in one request, a second request that names only
`retention.ms` left the topic with `retention.ms` alone and `cleanup.policy` back at its default; a request with an
**empty** entry array resets every option of the topic. A caller that wants to change one option reads the current
configuration with DescribeConfigs and sends it back with that option changed, which is what
`Admin\Config::nonDefaultValues()` exists for.

`ValidateOnly` runs the validation and writes nothing; a validated request answers 0 and leaves the topic untouched.

**A 0.11 broker alters topics and nothing else.** A broker resource - the one KIP-133 defines the wire format for -
falls into the `case resourceType =>` branch of `AdminManager.alterConfigs` and is answered with **42** and
`AlterConfigs is only supported for topics, but resource type is BROKER`, with `ValidateOnly` set or not. Dynamic
broker configuration arrived with Kafka 1.1 (KIP-226).

Error codes observed on the container:

| Code | Name                       | Request that produces it                    | ErrorMessage                                                     |
|------|----------------------------|---------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 0    | None                       | The configuration was replaced or validated | `null`                                                            |
| -1   | Unknown                    | A topic that does not exist                 | `Topic "t5-no-such-topic" does not exist.`                        |
| -1   | Unknown                    | A `null` config value                       | `null`                                                            |
| 29   | TopicAuthorizationFailed   | The client may describe but not alter it    | `null`                                                            |
| 31   | ClusterAuthorizationFailed | The client may not alter a broker resource  | `null`                                                            |
| 40   | InvalidConfig              | An unknown option **name**                  | `Unknown topic config name: no.such.option`                       |
| 42   | InvalidRequest             | An unparsable option **value**              | `Invalid config value for resource Resource(type=TOPIC, name='t5-vectors'}: Invalid value soon for configuration retention.ms: Not a number of type LONG` |
| 42   | InvalidRequest             | A resource type other than topic            | `AlterConfigs is only supported for topics, but resource type is BROKER` |

**Quirk: an unparsable VALUE is 42 here and -1 in CreateTopics.** Both apis end in the same `ConfigException` of the
common config framework, which is not an `ApiException`; `AdminManager.createTopics` lets it fall through to
`Errors.forException`, which answers **-1**, while `AdminManager.alterConfigs` catches it explicitly and re-throws
it as an `InvalidRequestException` - so the same wrong value is **42** through AlterConfigs and **-1** through
CreateTopics. The message of the 42 quotes `Resource.toString()` @ 0.11.0.3, which opens with `(` and closes with
`}`.

**Quirk: an unknown option NAME is 40, and its message changed with 0.11.** `LogConfig.validateNames` throws Kafka's
own `InvalidConfigurationException`, which is an `ApiException` and maps to 40 - the same code CreateTopics answers,
but the message reads `Unknown topic config name: no.such.option` where a 0.10.2.2 broker said
`Unknown Log configuration no.such.option.`.

**Quirk: a `null` config value is an NPE, reported as -1 with a null message.** The schema declares the value
nullable, but `AdminManager.alterConfigs` copies every entry into a `java.util.Properties` with
`setProperty(name, value)`, which throws a `NullPointerException` on a null. The resource comes back with the error
code **-1** and no message at all. An option is reset to its default by **leaving it out** of the request, never by
sending a null.

**Quirk: altering a topic that does not exist is -1, not 3.** `AdminUtils.changeTopicConfig` checks the topic first
and throws an `AdminOperationException`, which is not an `ApiException` either: the resource is answered with **-1**
and the message `Topic "…" does not exist.`.

## InitProducerId API (key 22, v0)

Hands out the **producer id** and the **epoch** that the record batches of an idempotent or transactional producer
carry. The api arrived with Kafka **0.11** (KIP-98); a 0.11.0.3 broker serves **v0** only.

```
InitProducerIdRequest => TransactionalId TransactionTimeoutMs
  TransactionalId      => nullable_string
  TransactionTimeoutMs => int32
```

```
InitProducerIdResponse => ThrottleTimeMs ErrorCode ProducerId ProducerEpoch
  ThrottleTimeMs => int32
  ErrorCode      => int16
  ProducerId     => int64
  ProducerEpoch  => int16
```

The api was born after KIP-124, so its answer starts with a `ThrottleTimeMs` like every other api of 0.11; there is
no version of it without one.

**`TransactionalId` decides everything about this request**, including which broker may answer it:

* **`null` — the idempotent producer.** `TransactionCoordinator.handleInitProducerId` @ 0.11.0.3 "blindly accepts"
  the request: it takes the next id out of the block the broker reserved in ZooKeeper (`ProducerIdManager`, blocks
  of 1000 ids, the next block written to `/latest_producer_id_block`) and answers it with the **epoch 0**. Nothing
  about that id is coordinated, so **any** broker of the cluster answers, and every call answers a *new* id — there
  is no way to ask for the id of a previous session again.
* **A non-empty id — the transactional producer.** The request has to reach the **transaction coordinator** of the
  id, the broker that leads the partition of `__transaction_state` the id hashes to, which the GroupCoordinator api
  with `CoordinatorType = 1` names. The answer is the producer id that `__transaction_state` holds for the id, with
  an epoch **one higher** than the one the previous producer of that id had — the bump is what fences that producer.
  Verified: two requests for `t7-idempotent-txn` in a row answer the same producer id with the epochs 0 and 1, and a
  batch written with the epoch 0 afterwards is refused with 47.

`TransactionTimeoutMs` is how long the coordinator waits for a status update of an open transaction before it
aborts it. **It is only looked at for a non-null transactional id**: the null branch of `handleInitProducerId`
returns before the check, so a 0.11.0.3 broker answers a `transaction_timeout_ms` of 999999999 next to a null id
with a perfectly normal producer id, while the same value next to a transactional id is answered with **50**
(`InvalidTransactionTimeout`), because it is above the broker's `transaction.max.timeout.ms` (900000 by default).

The **empty string** is not a transactional id: it is answered with **42** (`InvalidRequest`), deliberately, "to
make TransactionCoordinator's behavior consistent with producer client", which refuses an empty
`transactional.id` in its configuration.

Error codes observed on the 0.11.0.3 container:

| Code | Name                         | Request that produces it                                                  |
|------|------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 0    | None                         | A producer id and its epoch                                                |
| 15   | GroupCoordinatorNotAvailable | The very first lookup of a transactional id, while `__transaction_state` is being created |
| 16   | NotCoordinatorForGroup       | A transactional id sent to a broker that does not lead its `__transaction_state` partition — including **every** broker before the topic exists |
| 42   | InvalidRequest               | The empty string as a transactional id                                     |
| 50   | InvalidTransactionTimeout    | A `TransactionTimeoutMs` above `transaction.max.timeout.ms`, with a transactional id |
| 51   | ConcurrentTransactions       | The coordinator is fencing the previous epoch of the id; retry after a back-off |

An answer that carries an error carries **-1 as the producer id and -1 as the epoch**, the "no producer state" that
a record batch writes.

`Client::initProducerId(?string $transactionalId = null, int $transactionTimeoutMs = 60000)` sends the request to a
broker of the cluster for a null id and to `Client::getTransactionCoordinator()` for a real one, and answers with a
`Producer\Internals\ProducerIdAndEpoch`. What the client then does with the two values is in "The idempotent
producer" above.

## AddPartitionsToTxn API (key 24, v0)

Enrols topic-partitions into the open transaction of a producer. Kafka **0.11** (KIP-98), **v0** only.

```
AddPartitionsToTxnRequest => TransactionalId ProducerId ProducerEpoch [Topics]
  TransactionalId => string
  ProducerId      => int64
  ProducerEpoch   => int16
  Topics          => Topic [Partition]
    Topic     => string
    Partition => int32
```

```
AddPartitionsToTxnResponse => ThrottleTimeMs [Errors]
  ThrottleTimeMs  => int32
  Errors          => Topic [PartitionErrors]
    Topic           => string
    PartitionErrors => Partition ErrorCode
      Partition => int32
      ErrorCode => int16
```

The request goes to the **transaction coordinator** of the transactional id and has to be answered **before the
first Produce request** that writes into one of the partitions it names: the list of partitions the coordinator
keeps is the one it walks when the transaction ends, to write a COMMIT or an ABORT marker into each of them, and a
partition that is missing from it would keep its records uncommitted for ever. The **first** call of a transaction
is also what starts it - `TransactionCoordinator.handleAddPartitionsToTransaction` moves the id into `Ongoing` and
stamps it with the start time that `transaction.timeout.ms` is counted from.

Adding a partition twice is not an error; the coordinator answers a partition it already holds with the code 0.

**There is no top-level error code.** Everything the coordinator has to say - including the errors that are about
the *transactional id* rather than about a partition - is repeated on **every** partition of the answer, because
`KafkaApis.handleAddPartitionToTxnRequest` maps the single error of the coordinator over the requested partitions.
The array is called `errors` and its inner one `partition_errors` although both also carry the partitions that
succeeded.

| Code | Name | Request that produces it |
|------|------|---------------------------|
| 0    | None                         | The partition is part of the transaction |
| 15   | GroupCoordinatorNotAvailable | The coordinator of the id is not available on this broker |
| 16   | NotCoordinatorForGroup       | Another broker coordinates this transactional id |
| 29   | TopicAuthorizationFailed     | The client may not `Write` the topic |
| 47   | InvalidProducerEpoch         | An epoch below the one `__transaction_state` holds - **verified**: a second `InitProducerId` for the id bumps the epoch, and the producer that kept the old one is answered 47 per partition |
| 48   | InvalidTxnState              | The id is in a state that may not add partitions |
| 49   | InvalidProducerIdMapping     | The producer id is not the one the coordinator holds for the id |
| 51   | ConcurrentTransactions       | The previous transaction of the id is still being completed; retry after a back-off |
| 55   | OperationNotAttempted        | Another partition of the same request was unauthorized, so this one was not even tried |

**55 is the code of this api and of no other.** `KafkaApis` answers a request in which any partition is
unauthorized with `OperationNotAttempted` for every partition it did not look at, so that a client cannot conclude
from a missing error that a partition was added.

`Client::addPartitionsToTxn(Node $coordinator, string $transactionalId, ProducerIdAndEpoch $idAndEpoch, array $topicPartitions)`
sends it and reports the first error code the answer carries;
`Producer\Internals\TransactionManager::maybeAddPartitionsToTransaction()` calls it once per flush, for the
partitions of the batch the coordinator does not hold yet.

## AddOffsetsToTxn API (key 25, v0)

Enrols the offsets of a consumer group into the open transaction. Kafka **0.11** (KIP-98), **v0** only.

```
AddOffsetsToTxnRequest => TransactionalId ProducerId ProducerEpoch GroupId
  TransactionalId => string
  ProducerId      => int64
  ProducerEpoch   => int16
  GroupId         => string        # consumer_group_id
```

```
AddOffsetsToTxnResponse => ThrottleTimeMs ErrorCode
  ThrottleTimeMs => int32
  ErrorCode      => int16
```

The first half of `sendOffsetsToTransaction()`. It goes to the **transaction coordinator**, and it is
`AddPartitionsToTxn` for exactly one partition: `TransactionCoordinator.handleAddPartitionsToTransaction` @
0.11.0.3 is literally the method that serves it, called with
`new TopicPartition(GROUP_METADATA_TOPIC_NAME, partitionFor(consumerGroupId))`. That is why it has **one**
error code and no array - there is nothing to report per partition.

The offsets are not in this request; they travel in the `TxnOffsetCommit` that follows it, to the **group**
coordinator. The split is what makes the commit atomic with the produced records: the group coordinator writes
them as transactional records, and only the COMMIT marker that this request put the partition on the list for
makes them visible.

| Code | Name | Meaning |
|------|------|----------|
| 0    | None                               | `__consumer_offsets` of that group is part of the transaction |
| 15   | GroupCoordinatorNotAvailable       | The transaction coordinator is not available on this broker |
| 16   | NotCoordinatorForGroup             | Another broker coordinates this transactional id |
| 30   | GroupAuthorizationFailed           | The client may not `Read` the consumer group |
| 47   | InvalidProducerEpoch               | The epoch is below the one `__transaction_state` holds |
| 48   | InvalidTxnState                    | The id is in a state that may not add partitions |
| 49   | InvalidProducerIdMapping           | The producer id is not the one the coordinator holds for the id |
| 51   | ConcurrentTransactions             | The previous transaction of the id is still being completed |
| 53   | TransactionalIdAuthorizationFailed | The client may not `Write` the transactional id |

`Client::addOffsetsToTxn(Node $coordinator, string $transactionalId, ProducerIdAndEpoch $idAndEpoch, string $groupId)`.

## EndTxn API (key 26, v0)

Commits or aborts the open transaction. Kafka **0.11** (KIP-98), **v0** only.

```
EndTxnRequest => TransactionalId ProducerId ProducerEpoch TransactionResult
  TransactionalId   => string
  ProducerId        => int64
  ProducerEpoch     => int16
  TransactionResult => boolean      # 1 = COMMIT, 0 = ABORT
```

```
EndTxnResponse => ThrottleTimeMs ErrorCode
  ThrottleTimeMs => int32
  ErrorCode      => int16
```

The last request of a transaction, sent to the **transaction coordinator**. A single boolean byte is the whole
difference between a commit and an abort.

**The answer does not mean that the transaction is visible.** The coordinator answers as soon as it has written
`PrepareCommit`/`PrepareAbort` into `__transaction_state`; what follows is broker business - a
`WriteTxnMarkers` request to the leader of every partition of the transaction, each of them appending the control
batch, and then the `CompleteCommit`/`CompleteAbort` entry. A `read_committed` consumer sees the records a moment
*after* this call returned, which is what moves the last stable offset of the partitions past them.

Two behaviours of a 0.11.0.3 coordinator that the state table of `TransactionCoordinator.handleEndTransaction`
explains and that were verified on the container:

* **A repeated commit is answered with 0.** An id in `CompleteCommit` that is asked to commit again is a no-op
  success, so a producer that lost the answer of its `EndTxn` may simply send it again.
* **An abort after a commit is 48.** The same id asked to *abort* while it is in `CompleteCommit` is an illegal
  state transition, `InvalidTxnState`.

| Code | Name | Meaning |
|------|------|----------|
| 0    | None                               | The coordinator decided the outcome |
| 15   | GroupCoordinatorNotAvailable       | The transaction coordinator is not available on this broker |
| 16   | NotCoordinatorForGroup             | Another broker coordinates this transactional id |
| 47   | InvalidProducerEpoch               | The epoch is below the one `__transaction_state` holds - fenced |
| 48   | InvalidTxnState                    | There is no transaction in a state this result may be applied to |
| 49   | InvalidProducerIdMapping           | The producer id is not the one the coordinator holds for the id |
| 51   | ConcurrentTransactions             | The previous transaction of the id is still being completed |
| 53   | TransactionalIdAuthorizationFailed | The client may not `Write` the transactional id |

`Client::endTxn(Node $coordinator, string $transactionalId, ProducerIdAndEpoch $idAndEpoch, bool $transactionResult)`
with `EndTxnRequest::COMMIT` or `EndTxnRequest::ABORT`.

## WriteTxnMarkers API (key 27, v0)

Writes the control batches of a finished transaction into the partitions of it. Kafka **0.11** (KIP-98), **v0**
only, **broker to broker**.

```
WriteTxnMarkersRequest => [TransactionMarkers]
  TransactionMarkers => ProducerId ProducerEpoch TransactionResult [Topics] CoordinatorEpoch
    ProducerId        => int64
    ProducerEpoch     => int16
    TransactionResult => boolean
    Topics            => Topic [Partition]
      Topic     => string
      Partition => int32
    CoordinatorEpoch  => int32
```

```
WriteTxnMarkersResponse => [TransactionMarkers]
  TransactionMarkers => ProducerId [Topics]
    ProducerId => int64
    Topics     => Topic [Partitions]
      Topic      => string
      Partitions => Partition ErrorCode
        Partition => int32
        ErrorCode => int16
```

This is the request a **transaction coordinator** sends to the leader of every partition of a transaction after an
`EndTxn`, and it is what makes that leader append the control batch a `read_committed` consumer reads the outcome
from. `TransactionMarkerChannelManager` @ 0.11.0.3 groups the pending markers by destination broker - one entry per
producer, so one request may complete several transactions at once - and repeats them until every partition
answered without a retriable error; only then is `CompleteCommit`/`CompleteAbort` written.

**No client ever sends it**, and this line implements the classes for completeness of the api block, not for a
client method. Two peculiarities:

* **It is the only answer of Kafka 0.11 without a `throttle_time_ms`.** `WRITE_TXN_MARKERS_RESPONSE_V0` in
  `Protocol.java` starts with the array; the quotas of KIP-124 throttle clients, and this api has none.
* `CoordinatorEpoch` is the epoch of the `__transaction_state` partition the *sender* owns, not the epoch of the
  producer. A leader that has already seen a higher one refuses the marker with **52**
  (`TransactionCoordinatorFenced`), which is how a coordinator that lost its partition is kept from writing markers
  behind the back of the one that took it over.

`KafkaApis.handleWriteTxnMarkersRequest` requires `ClusterAction` on the cluster, which is only granted to the
broker principal on a *secured* cluster; the container of this branch runs without an authorizer, so it serves the
request for anybody - which is what makes a wire vector of it possible at all, and it really does append a COMMIT
control batch into the partition that the vector names.

| Code | Name | Meaning |
|------|------|----------|
| 0    | None                         | The control batch was appended |
| 3    | UnknownTopicOrPartition      | The broker does not host that partition |
| 6    | NotLeaderForPartition        | The broker does not lead it any more |
| 31   | ClusterAuthorizationFailed   | The client may not perform a `ClusterAction` |
| 43   | UnsupportedForMessageFormat  | The `message.format.version` of the topic is below 0.11.0 and has no place for a control batch |
| 52   | TransactionCoordinatorFenced | The `CoordinatorEpoch` of the marker is below the one the leader saw |

An empty marker array is answered with an empty array, which is what the api probe of this branch sends to check
that the key is served at all.

## TxnOffsetCommit API (key 28, v0)

Commits consumer offsets inside a transaction. Kafka **0.11** (KIP-98), **v0** only.

```
TxnOffsetCommitRequest => TransactionalId GroupId ProducerId ProducerEpoch [Topics]
  TransactionalId => string
  GroupId         => string        # consumer_group_id
  ProducerId      => int64
  ProducerEpoch   => int16
  Topics          => Topic [Partitions]
    Topic      => string
    Partitions => Partition Offset Metadata
      Partition => int32
      Offset    => int64
      Metadata  => nullable_string
```

```
TxnOffsetCommitResponse => ThrottleTimeMs [Topics]
  ThrottleTimeMs => int32
  Topics         => Topic [Partitions]
    Topic      => string
    Partitions => Partition ErrorCode
      Partition => int32
      ErrorCode => int16
```

The second half of `sendOffsetsToTransaction()` and **the one request of the transaction protocol that goes to the
group coordinator**, because that is the broker which owns `__consumer_offsets`. It has to follow an
`AddOffsetsToTxn` for the same group; without it the group coordinator answers **48**, the code of a transactional
write into a partition that is not part of an open transaction.

What it writes is an ordinary offset commit **inside a transactional record batch** stamped with the producer id
and the epoch of this request. Those records are invisible to an OffsetFetch of the group until the transaction
coordinator has written the COMMIT marker into that partition; an aborted transaction leaves them in the log with
an ABORT marker behind them and the group keeps the offset it had before.

Compared with `OffsetCommit` the request has **no generation id, no member id and no retention time**: the
producer that commits the offsets is not a member of the group at all, and the fencing a generation would give is
done by the producer epoch instead.

As in `AddPartitionsToTxn` there is **no top-level error code**; the state of the group and of the transaction is
repeated on every partition of the answer.

| Code | Name | Meaning |
|------|------|----------|
| 0    | None                         | The offset is in `__consumer_offsets`, invisible until the commit |
| 12   | OffsetMetadataTooLarge       | The metadata is above `offset.metadata.max.bytes` |
| 14   | GroupLoadInProgress          | The coordinator is still reading the group out of the log |
| 15   | GroupCoordinatorNotAvailable | The group coordinator is not available on this broker |
| 16   | NotCoordinatorForGroup       | Another broker coordinates this group |
| 30   | GroupAuthorizationFailed     | The client may not `Read` the group |
| 43   | UnsupportedForMessageFormat  | `__consumer_offsets` runs a `message.format.version` below 0.11.0 |
| 47   | InvalidProducerEpoch         | The epoch is below the one the log holds for the producer id |
| 48   | InvalidTxnState              | The offsets partition is not part of an open transaction of this producer |

`Client::txnOffsetCommit(Node $groupCoordinator, string $transactionalId, string $groupId, ProducerIdAndEpoch $idAndEpoch, array $topicPartitionOffsets)`.

## Transport security (SSL)

Kafka 0.9.0.0 replaced the single `port` of a broker with a list of **listeners**, one per security protocol
(`listeners=PLAINTEXT://0.0.0.0:9092,SSL://0.0.0.0:9093`; the container of this branch adds
`SASL_PLAINTEXT://0.0.0.0:9094,SASL_SSL://0.0.0.0:9095`). `SocketServer.scala` @ 0.10.2.2 opens one acceptor per
entry and remembers the listener of every connection; from there on the listeners are indistinguishable, they run
the same `KafkaApis` over the same request set - the two SASL ones only insert the authentication of the
"SASL/PLAIN" subsection before the first request. **No request or response changes a single byte because of the
transport**, which is why nothing above `IO\SocketStream` in this client knows that encryption exists.

A broker speaks TLS from the very first byte of a connection to its SSL listener: there is no in-protocol upgrade,
no `STARTTLS`-like request, and a plaintext request sent to 9093 is just an unparseable TLS record - the broker
drops the socket without an answer (verified: `tests/Integration/SslTransportTest.php`).

### Client configuration

| Option | Default | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `security.protocol` | `PLAINTEXT` | `PLAINTEXT`, `SSL`, `SASL_PLAINTEXT`, `SASL_SSL`; the two SASL ones add the authentication of the "SASL/PLAIN" subsection below |
| `ssl.protocol` | `TLS` | `TLS`, `TLSv1_1`, `TLSv1_2`, `SSL`, `SSLv2`, `SSLv3` - the names of the Java client |
| `ssl.enabled.protocols` | - | list of the same values; when set it replaces `ssl.protocol` entirely |
| `ssl.ca.cert.location` | - | PEM bundle used as the trust anchor (`ssl.truststore.location` in Java) |
| `ssl.client.cert.location` | - | client certificate, only for `ssl.client.auth=required` brokers |
| `ssl.key.location` | - | private key of the client certificate |
| `ssl.key.password` | - | passphrase of that key |

`SocketStream` connects the TCP socket first and calls `stream_socket_enable_crypto()` on it afterwards, with the
crypto method that `ssl.enabled.protocols`/`ssl.protocol` map to. The stream context always carries
`verify_peer`, `verify_peer_name` and `peer_name`, so the certificate of the broker is verified and has to match
the host that was dialled; a failed handshake becomes a `NetworkException` carrying the OpenSSL message.

Two properties of PHP are worth recording, because neither is obvious:

* the handshake is bounded by the **connection timeout** the stream was created with, not by `request.timeout.ms` -
  the OpenSSL loop of PHP keeps the deadline the socket was opened with;
* `TLSv1_1` alone is refused by a current OpenSSL build (`no protocols available`) even though the broker offers
  it, because the default security level of OpenSSL 3 rejects TLS below 1.2. `TLS` (the default) and `TLSv1_2`
  negotiate TLSv1.2 against a default-configured 0.10.2.2 broker.

### Metadata answers with the endpoint of the listener it arrived on

Every version of the Metadata api - v0, v1 and v2 alike - carries exactly **one** `Host`/`Port` pair per broker, so
a single response cannot describe more than one listener. `KafkaApis.handleTopicMetadataRequest` @ 0.10.2.2 resolves
that with the listener the request came in on:

```scala
val responseBody = new MetadataResponse(
  brokers.map(_.getNode(request.listenerName)).asJava, clusterId, ..., requestVersion)
```

and `getTopicMetadata(authorizedTopics, request.listenerName, errorUnavailableEndpoints)` does the same for the
leader and replica endpoints. (The 0.9.0.1 broker of the line below did it with `request.securityProtocol`, which is
the same thing as long as a broker binds one listener per security protocol.)

Verified against the container of this branch, which advertises
`PLAINTEXT://127.0.0.1:9092,SSL://127.0.0.1:9093,SASL_PLAINTEXT://127.0.0.1:9094,SASL_SSL://127.0.0.1:9095`: the
identical Metadata request answers with broker `#0` at `127.0.0.1:9092` on the plaintext connection, at
`127.0.0.1:9093` on the encrypted one, and at `127.0.0.1:9094` / `127.0.0.1:9095` on the two authenticated ones.

The consequence for a client is that **the security protocol is a property of the whole client, not of a single
connection**: a client that bootstraps over TLS discovers TLS endpoints and keeps its configuration for every
connection it opens to them, and there is no way for it to fall back to plaintext by accident - the plaintext port
never appears in what it learned. This client gets that for free because the same configuration array reaches
`Cluster` -> `Node::getConnection()` -> `ConnectionFactory` -> `SocketStream`; the connection cache is keyed by the
security protocol as well, so a plaintext and an encrypted connection to one `host:port` can never be confused.

### SASL/PLAIN

Kafka 0.9 already shipped SASL, but only the GSSAPI (Kerberos) mechanism, and the exchange happened *outside* the
protocol: the broker expected the raw token exchange on a freshly opened connection, with nothing framing it and no
way to negotiate a mechanism. **Kafka 0.10.0 (KIP-43) put the negotiation inside the protocol** with the
`SaslHandshake` request (api key 17, v0) and added the PLAIN mechanism, which is what makes SASL implementable in
pure PHP: PLAIN is a single token carrying a user name and a password, no Kerberos ticket in sight.

A `SASL_PLAINTEXT` or `SASL_SSL` connection therefore has three phases (`SaslServerAuthenticator.authenticate()` @
0.10.2.2, states `HANDSHAKE_REQUEST -> AUTHENTICATE -> COMPLETE`):

```
client                                     broker (SASL_PLAINTEXT://9094, SASL_SSL://9095)
  |                                                                 |
  |-- TCP connect ------------------------------------------------->|
  |-- TLS handshake (SASL_SSL only) ------------------------------->|
  |                                                                 |
  |-- Size ApiKey=17 ApiVersion=0 CorrelationId ClientId "PLAIN" -->|   an ordinary Kafka request
  |<-- Size CorrelationId ErrorCode=0 EnabledMechanisms=["PLAIN"] --|   an ordinary Kafka response
  |                                                                 |
  |-- Size "\0<username>\0<password>" ----------------------------->|   NOT a request: no header at all
  |<-- Size=0 ------------------------------------------------------|   the empty token, exchange complete
  |                                                                 |
  |-- Metadata, Produce, Fetch, ... ------------------------------->|   the connection is now ordinary
```

Only the **handshake** is a Kafka request. The tokens after it are bare frames: an `INT32` length followed by that
many bytes, which is precisely the `bytes` primitive of the protocol - no api key, no api version, no correlation
id, no client id. The broker reads them with a plain `NetworkReceive` and hands the payload to
`SaslServer.evaluateResponse()`; the `SaslAuthenticate` request (api key 36) that wraps the very same tokens into a
framed request with an error code is **Kafka 1.0** and must not be sent to a 0.10 broker.

For PLAIN the exchange is one round trip. The client token is the RFC 4616 message `authzid \0 authcid \0 passwd`,
which `PlainSaslServer.evaluateResponse()` splits on the NUL bytes into exactly three parts - this client always
sends an empty authorization id, as the Java `PlainLoginModule` does - and the answer is
`new byte[0]`, i.e. the four bytes `00 00 00 00` of an empty frame. The credentials travel in clear text inside the
token, so `SASL_PLAINTEXT` is only sound on a trusted network; `SASL_SSL` performs the identical exchange inside
the TLS channel and is the same code path plus the `ssl.*` options.

#### Client configuration

| Option | Default | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `security.protocol` | `PLAINTEXT` | `SASL_PLAINTEXT` and `SASL_SSL` authenticate; `SASL_SSL` also takes every `ssl.*` option |
| `sasl.mechanism` | `PLAIN` | the only implemented mechanism, see below |
| `sasl.username` | - | the `authcid` of the token, `user_<name>` in the JAAS file of the broker |
| `sasl.password` | - | sent in clear text inside the token |

The JAAS configuration of the Java client (`sasl.jaas.config`, or a `KafkaClient` login module named by
`java.security.auth.login.config`) is **not** reproduced: a login module is a Java class, and PHP has no JAAS. The
two options above are the whole equivalent of the `username`/`password` of `PlainLoginModule`. An empty or missing
one is refused before a socket is opened, because a broker would only answer it by dropping the connection.

Of the four mechanisms a 0.10.2.2 broker can enable, this client performs `PLAIN` only. `GSSAPI` needs a Kerberos
binding, which PHP has neither in core nor as a maintained extension that could produce tokens a broker accepts;
`SCRAM-SHA-256` and `SCRAM-SHA-512`, added by 0.10.2 (KIP-84), need the multi-round exchange of RFC 5802 with
SASLprep-normalized credentials, which is a mechanism of its own and is out of scope here. Both are refused with an
`InvalidConfigurationException` that names the reason rather than being sent to the broker.

#### What the broker does, observed on `docker/kafka-0.10.2.2`

Every one of these was observed on the running container, and all but the version probe of the last-but-one row are
asserted by `tests/Integration/SaslTransportTest.php`. None of them produces an error code, because before Kafka 1.0
the only error code of the whole exchange is the one of the handshake response:

| Situation | Broker behaviour |
|---|---|
| handshake with an enabled mechanism | `ErrorCode = 0`, `EnabledMechanisms = ["PLAIN"]` |
| handshake with a mechanism it has not enabled | `ErrorCode = 33` (UnsupportedSaslMechanism) with the enabled ones, **then the connection is closed** |
| token with a wrong password, or for an unknown user | the connection is **closed during the token exchange**, without any answer (`SaslException` -> `IOException`) |
| any request other than the handshake as the first frame | the connection is closed without an answer - the broker parses the first frame as a handshake (or as a GSSAPI token, see below) and gives up |
| a second `SaslHandshake` on the same connection | the connection is closed: the state machine is past `HANDSHAKE_REQUEST` |
| `SaslHandshake` **v1** | the connection is closed - a 0.10.2.2 broker serves version 0 only |
| `ApiVersions` (api key 18) before the handshake | **answered normally**, with the full api-version list; it is the one request a 0.10.2 broker serves before authentication (KIP-35), so a client may probe the versions of a broker it has not authenticated to |

A client can therefore not tell a rejected password from a broker that died, which is why this client raises a
`SaslAuthenticationException` (client-side, no wire code) instead of the bare `NetworkException` of the dropped
socket: retrying it is pointless. The dedicated error code 58 (SaslAuthenticationFailed) arrived with Kafka 1.0.

The first frame of a connection is ambiguous on purpose: `SaslServerAuthenticator` @ 0.10.2.2 accepts either a
`SaslHandshake` request or a raw GSSAPI token there (`GSSAPI_OR_HANDSHAKE_REQUEST`), so that a 0.9 client, which
knows nothing about the handshake, can still authenticate with Kerberos against a 0.10 broker. That is why an
unparseable first frame is dropped silently rather than answered with an error: the broker cannot know whether it
was a malformed request or a token of a mechanism it does not have.

The endpoint property of the section above holds for the SASL listeners as well: the identical Metadata request
answers with `127.0.0.1:9094` on a `SASL_PLAINTEXT` connection and with `127.0.0.1:9095` on a `SASL_SSL` one, so a
client that bootstraps over SASL discovers the SASL endpoints of the cluster and keeps authenticating to every
broker it learns about. The connection cache is keyed by the security protocol **and** the user name, so two
clients with different credentials never share a connection.

## Cluster readiness

**This is one of the things Kafka 0.10 quietly fixed.** On the two lines below, a broker that had just started
answered a Metadata request with an **empty broker array** and kept doing so until some topic existed: the broker
list of a Metadata response comes from the metadata cache of the broker, that cache is only filled once the
controller has pushed an `UpdateMetadata` request to it, and on a cluster without a single topic that never
happened. Clients had to treat an empty broker array as "not ready, retry", never as "the cluster has no brokers".

A **0.10.2.2** broker fills its cache with the live brokers as soon as the controller has elected itself, with or
without topics. `KafkaApis.handleTopicMetadataRequest` @ 0.10.2.2 answers with `metadataCache.getAliveBrokers`, and
that is populated from the very first `UpdateMetadata` the controller sends on broker startup. Verified on a
throwaway container of the image of this branch, started empty and asked before anything else touched it:

```
Metadata v0, empty topic array:  brokers = 1, topics = 0
Metadata v1, null topic array:   brokers = 1, topics = 0
```

Two consequences, and neither of them makes the readiness probe obsolete:

* An empty broker array is **still** not "the cluster has no brokers" — it is "this broker has not caught up yet",
  and a broker that is still starting, or one whose controller connection has just been re-established, can answer
  it for a moment. `tests/Fixture/ClusterReadinessProbe.php` and `Cluster::bootstrap()` keep retrying it, and the
  probe costs nothing when the first answer is already complete. The probe asks with **version 0** on purpose: it
  is the first frame the suite sends to a broker that may still be booting, every release from 0.8 on serves that
  version, and nothing the later ones add is of any use before the cluster is up. `Cluster::bootstrap()` asks with
  version 2 instead — with the one topic it was given, or with a `null` topic array when it was given none.
* A **fresh topic** still answers 5 (`LeaderNotAvailable`) or 6 (`NotLeaderForPartition`) for a moment after it has
  been created, exactly as on 0.8 and 0.9, because the leader election is asynchronous. Every helper that creates a
  topic retries those two codes.

Note that readiness is not covered by a TCP health check either: the broker accepts connections on 9092 long before
it has registered itself with ZooKeeper.

## Broker quirks and observations

Everything a Kafka 0.11.0.3 broker does that a client cannot read out of the protocol grammar, collected from the
integration suite of this branch. The section that describes each item in detail is named behind it. An item marked
**(0.11)** is a change against the 0.10.2.2 line, one marked **(0.10)** a change that line made against 0.9.0.1 and
that 0.11 keeps; the older lists are in `docs/protocol/0.10.2.md` and `docs/protocol/0.9.0.md` on their branches.

**Framing and api surface**

* **(0.10) An api the broker cannot parse costs the connection.** The broker closes the socket - `docker logs
  kafka-0-11-0-3` shows `Closing socket for ... because of error` with the `InvalidRequestException` or
  `SchemaException` that caused it - where a 0.9.0.1 broker dropped the frame and kept the connection open, and a
  0.8.2.2 broker closed it. A **malformed body of a version the broker does serve** is punished the same way.
  → "An api the broker does not serve closes the connection".
* **(0.11) The version that costs the connection moved with the release.** A 0.10.2.2 broker hung up on
  OffsetCommit v3, Metadata v3, JoinGroup v2 and the rest of the 0.11 versions; a 0.11.0.3 broker serves all of them
  and hangs up one version higher (OffsetCommit v4, Metadata v5, JoinGroup v3, Fetch v6 …), and on the api key
  **34**. The reason it reports in the log changed too: `java.lang.IllegalArgumentException: Invalid version for API
  key OFFSET_COMMIT: 4` out of `ApiKeys.schemaFor()`, where 0.10.2.2 named `Protocol.CURR_VERSION`.
  → "An api the broker does not serve closes the connection".
* **(0.10) ApiVersions (18) is the one api whose unknown version is answered**, with the error code 35 and an empty
  api array, in the version 0 layout, on a connection that stays open. **(0.11)** That answer is still the *version
  0* layout although the broker serves version 1: `ApiVersionsResponse.unsupportedVersionSend()` hard-codes the
  version `0`, so the frame has no throttle time and is 6 bytes of body, exactly as it was on 0.10.
  → "ApiVersions API (key 18, v0 and v1)".
* **(0.11) ApiVersions v1 appends its throttle time instead of prepending it.** Every other api KIP-124 touched puts
  `throttle_time_ms` first; `API_VERSIONS_RESPONSE_V1` puts it last, so that the leading `error_code` stays at the
  offset a version 0 client reads it at. → "ApiVersions API (key 18, v0 and v1)".
* **(0.11) The reported api set depends on `inter.broker.protocol.version`.**
  `ApiVersionsResponse.createApiVersionsResponse()` filters `ApiKeys.values()` by
  `minRequiredInterBrokerMagic <= minMagic`, so a 0.11.0.3 broker rolled back to an older inter-broker version
  reports fewer than the 34 keys the container reports. → "API keys".
* **(0.11) The ACL apis 29, 30 and 31 are served but disabled.** Without an `authorizer.class.name` a 0.11.0.3
  broker answers all three with the error code **54** (`SecurityDisabled`) and the message
  `No Authorizer is configured on the broker` - with a trailing dot in the CreateAcls and DeleteAcls answer and
  without one in the DescribeAcls answer. → "API keys".
* **(0.11) `InitProducerId` (22) with a null `transactional_id` allocates a producer id and creates nothing else.**
  The broker answers error 0 with a fresh producer id and the epoch 0, straight out of the id block it reserved in
  ZooKeeper; no `__transaction_state` entry is written, which is what makes the api safe to probe. The
  `__transaction_state` topic is created by a `GroupCoordinator` (FindCoordinator) v1 request with
  `coordinator_type = 1`, not by this one.
* **(0.10) ControlledShutdown (7) is the last api that ignores its version.** It is still parsed by the Scala
  `ControlledShutdownRequest.readFrom()`, so the broker answers v0 (the header without a client id), v1 and
  anything above, although ApiVersions reports `MinVersion = MaxVersion = 1` for it. Every other api of the keys 0
  to 9 has lost that property since 0.9. → "The last Scala api does not check its version".
* **(0.10) SaslHandshake (17) on a non-SASL listener answers 34** (`IllegalSaslState`) with the enabled mechanisms,
  whatever mechanism was asked for: on a SASL listener the request never reaches the api layer, so everything
  `KafkaApis.handleSaslHandshakeRequest` sees is a handshake at the wrong moment.
* **(0.10) A broker without topics answers Metadata with its brokers**, where 0.8 and 0.9 answered zero brokers
  until a topic existed. An empty broker array is still "not ready, retry". → "Cluster readiness".
* **Metadata v0 answers with the endpoint of the listener the request arrived on**, one host/port per broker: a
  client that bootstraps over TLS learns the TLS endpoints of the whole cluster, one that bootstraps in plaintext
  learns the plaintext ones, and no request can ask one listener about the other.
  → "Metadata answers with the endpoint of the listener it arrived on".
* **An empty topic array means two different things.** In Metadata **v0** it asks for *every* topic of the cluster;
  in **v1** and above `[]` means "no topic at all" and `null` (`ff ff ff ff`) is what asks for every topic. A client
  that sends the v0 request unchanged as v1 gets an empty answer instead of the whole cluster.

**Message format and compression**

* **(0.10) The api version does not decide the format of the log, `message.format.version` of the topic does.** A
  magic 0 message set sent with Produce **v2** is accepted and stored as format v1 with the `Timestamp` -1, and a
  magic 1 set is accepted by a Produce v1 and even a Produce v0 request. The broker never checks one against the
  other. → "What the broker converts, and when".
* **(0.10) A Fetch below version 2 is answered down-converted to format v0**: the timestamps are stripped, the
  relative inner offsets of a compressed set are made absolute and the set is recompressed - an lz4 set then carries
  the *broken* frame checksum, because the result is a v0 set. Version 2 of the Fetch api is nothing but the
  statement "I understand message format v1". → "What the broker converts, and when".
* **(0.10) `message.timestamp.type=LogAppendTime` rewrites only the wrapper of a compressed batch.** Its attributes
  announce the codec and the LogAppendTime bit and its timestamp is the append time; the inner messages keep the
  `CreateTime` values the producer sent, and a reader has to ignore them for the whole set.
  → "Timestamps inside a compressed message set".
* **(0.10) A compressed v1 set stores relative inner offsets**, 0, 1, 2 …, and the wrapper carries the absolute
  offset of the **last** inner message, so the reader computes `wrapperOffset - lastInnerOffset + innerOffset`. A v0
  set stores absolute ones. → "Offsets inside a compressed message set".
* **(0.10) The lz4 frame of a magic 0 message carries a broken descriptor checksum** (KAFKA-3160), the one of a
  magic 1 message the correct one; both the broker and this client write the one that belongs to the magic byte and
  accept either when reading. → "The lz4 codec and the KAFKA-3160 checksum".
* **(0.11) The on-disk bytes of a record batch are its wire bytes.** The log segment of a partition that a
  `docker cp` copies out of the container is byte for byte what a Fetch v4 answers for the same offsets, header
  fields and checksum included - which is what makes `DumpLogSegments` a second opinion on every capture.
  → "RecordBatch (message format v2)".
* **(0.11) A `LogAppendTime` batch keeps the timestamps of its producer.** Only `MaxTimestamp` is replaced with the
  append time and only bit 3 of the attributes is set; `FirstTimestamp` and every `TimestampDelta` stay as they were
  produced, and a reader has to answer `MaxTimestamp` for all of the records instead of adding the deltas.
  → "Offsets, timestamps and sequence numbers are deltas".
* **(0.11) The lz4 frame of a record batch always carries the correct descriptor checksum** `0x82`: KAFKA-3160 is
  bound to the message format v0, which a magic 2 batch can never be. → "Compression compresses the records, not a
  wrapper".
* **(0.11) The broker converts a produced batch down on write and the log down on read.** A record batch v2 sent to
  a topic with `message.format.version=0.9.0` is accepted by a Produce v3 and stored as format v0, and the log of a
  0.11 topic is answered as format v1 to a Fetch v2/v3 and as format v0 to a Fetch v0/v1, one message per record.
  → "What the broker stores, and what it answers".
* **(0.11) `last_stable_offset` and `aborted_transactions` are only filled for `read_committed`.** A Fetch v4 with
  `isolation_level = 0` answers -1 and `null` for them even on a topic that has never seen a transaction; with
  `isolation_level = 1` the same partition answers the real LSO and an empty array. → "RecordBatch (message
  format v2)".
* **(0.11) Time-based retention deletes a segment by the largest timestamp it holds, not by its age.** A batch
  produced with a `CreateTime` far in the past - a fixed value in a test, for instance - is removed by the very next
  run of the retention check (`log.retention.check.interval.ms`, five minutes on this container), although it was
  appended seconds earlier. A capture has to be copied out immediately, and an integration test has to stamp its
  records with the current time. → "RecordBatch (message format v2)".

**Produce and Fetch**

* **(0.10) `LogAppendTime` of a Produce v2 answer is `-1` unless the topic asks for it**; on a
  `message.timestamp.type=LogAppendTime` topic it is the clock of the broker, and it is then the timestamp every
  message of that batch is fetched back with. → "Produce API (key 0, v0 to v3)".
* **(0.10) The request-level `MaxBytes` of Fetch v3 is spent in the order of the request.**
  `ReplicaManager.readFromLocalLog` subtracts what it has read from `limitBytes`, so a partition behind an exhausted
  budget comes back with an empty message set although its high water mark is above the fetch offset - and the same
  fetch with the partitions swapped serves the other one. An empty partition is therefore not proof that it is
  drained. → "Fetch API (key 1, v0 to v5)".
* **(0.10) The first non-empty partition of a v3 fetch always makes progress.** `minOneMessage = !hardMaxBytesLimit`
  and `hardMaxBytesLimit` is `versionId <= 2`, so a 4 KiB message is returned whole even for `MaxBytes = 0`: an
  answer may be *larger* than the limit it asked for. The consequence is that the `RecordTooLargeException` of the
  lower versions cannot happen for a v3 client. → "Fetch API (key 1, v0 to v5)".

**Offsets**

* **OffsetFetch v1 no longer validates the partition**: a partition the cluster does not host is answered with the
  offset `-1` and the error code `0` ("nothing committed"), where 0.8.2.2 answered 3. Version 0, which reads
  ZooKeeper, still answers 3. → "OffsetFetch API (key 9, v0 to v3)".
* **An oversized offset metadata never reaches the coordinator cache.** A commit whose metadata exceeds
  `offset.metadata.max.bytes` is answered 12 (`OffsetMetadataTooLarge`) and the entry is filtered out before the
  append *and* before the cache is updated, so an immediate OffsetFetch reports "never committed". A 0.8.2.2 broker
  cached it. Verified with a 5000 character metadata. → "OffsetCommit API (key 8, v0 to v3)".
* **`RetentionTime` of OffsetCommit v2 counts from the commit.** `-1` asks for `offsets.retention.minutes` of the
  broker, any other value replaces it for that commit alone, and the expiry stored in `__consumer_offsets` is
  always `commitTimestamp + retention` with the commit timestamp being the receive time of the broker. Expired
  entries disappear at the next run of `offsets.retention.check.interval.ms`, not at the instant of the expiry.
  → "OffsetCommit API (key 8, v0 to v3)".
* **(0.10) "No message with that timestamp" is not an error.** Offsets **v1** answers a timestamp above the last
  message of the partition - and any timestamp on an empty log - with the error code **0**, the timestamp `-1` and
  the offset **-1**; only a topic whose `message.format.version` is 0.9.0 answers **43**
  (`UnsupportedForMessageFormat`), and `-1`/`-2` keep working even there. A lookup resolves to the **inner** record
  of a compressed batch, a `LogAppendTime` topic is searched by the append time the broker stamped, and the same
  partition twice in one request is answered 42 (`InvalidRequest`).
  → "What a 0.10.2.2 broker answers (verified against the container)".
* **(0.10) The group-level error code of OffsetFetch v2 cannot be provoked on a one-broker cluster**, which
  coordinates every group itself: an unknown group id, an empty one and a 300-character one are all answered with
  the code 0 and an empty topic array, because `handleFetchOffsets` "returns offsets blindly regardless the current
  group state". → "The top-level error code of v2".
* **A null offset metadata comes back as an empty string**, not as `ff ff`: `OffsetMetadata.NoMetadata` is `""`, for
  v1 and v2 alike. The 0.8.2.2 capture of `offsetfetch.response.v1` still shows `ff ff`, so a client has to read
  both. → "A partition without a committed offset".

**Group membership**

* **JoinGroup blocks.** The coordinator answers a JoinGroup only once every known member has rejoined, so
  `request.timeout.ms` has to exceed `session.timeout.ms`, and two members of a group cannot be driven from one
  single-threaded process. → "Group membership protocol (keys 11 to 14)".
* **An error answer of JoinGroup carries the generation 0**, not the `-1` of the Java client's
  `UNKNOWN_GENERATION_ID`: `GroupCoordinator.joinError` builds it with `generationId = 0` (the docblocks
  of `main` are wrong about this). → "JoinGroup API (key 11, v0, v1 and v2)".
* **The `members` array of a JoinGroup answer is filled only for the leader**, and its order is the order of the
  coordinator's internal map, not the order in which the members joined. An assignor must therefore sort what it
  gets — both assignors of this client do. → "JoinGroup API (key 11, v0, v1 and v2)".
* **Heartbeat checks the group before the member**: an unknown member of a rebalancing group is answered 27
  (`RebalanceInProgress`), not 25 (`UnknownMemberId`), and while the coordinator is loading the group it answers 0
  to everything. → "Heartbeat API (key 12, v0 and v1)".
* **A member id is `<client id>-<uuid>`** and the first generation of a group is **1**.
  → "Group membership protocol (keys 11 to 14)".
* **(0.10) A group whose last member leaves survives as `Empty`.** It keeps its committed offsets until
